e:T586c,The earlier posts explained how the engine, the event loop, objects, async code and modules work. This one is about what happens when they misbehave in front of real users: a page that feels sluggish on a mid-range phone, a tab that grows to 2 GB over an afternoon, a Node service whose p99 latency doubles after a deploy, a stack trace that points at . The tools are mature: Core Web Vitals for what users feel, the Performance and Memory panels for why, V8's profilers for Node, source maps, and real-user monitoring for how often. The skill is using them in the right order, measuring before changing anything, and not being fooled by your own benchmark.

> Series map: 00 Engines & Runtime → 01 Event Loop & Concurrency → 02 Objects, Prototypes & Metaprogramming → 03 Async Engineering → 04 Modules, Packaging & TypeScript at Scale → 05 Performance, Memory & Production Debugging (this post) (future: see the extensions table at the end).

Learning Outcome

- State the Core Web Vitals thresholds, the percentile they are judged at, and the sub-parts of LCP and INP.- Collect field metrics with attribution, and explain why lab tools cannot measure INP.- Read a Performance panel recording: flame chart, self vs total time, long tasks, forced layouts.- Break up long tasks with  and find their causes with the Long Animation Frames API.- Find a memory leak with heap snapshots, the comparison view and the allocation timeline, and name the four usual retainers.- Profile Node in development and production: , , , heap snapshots on signal.- Ship source maps safely, capture production errors and RUM data, and avoid the classic benchmarking mistakes.

Chapter 1 — Core Web Vitals

Terminology

- Field data — measurements from real users (RUM, or Chrome's CrUX data set). Core Web Vitals are judged on field data.- Lab data — a synthetic run (Lighthouse, a DevTools recording) on one device and network. Good for debugging, not for judging.- p75 — the 75th percentile. A page "passes" a metric when at least 75% of page views are good, judged separately for mobile and desktop.- LCP — Largest Contentful Paint — when the largest image or text block in the viewport was rendered.- INP — Interaction to Next Paint — roughly the worst latency from a click, tap or key press to the next frame, over the whole page visit. It replaced FID as a Core Web Vital on 12 March 2024.- CLS — Cumulative Layout Shift — the largest burst of unexpected layout shifts. Shifts are grouped into session windows of at most 5 s, with gaps under 1 s. Shifts within 500 ms of user input do not count.

Thresholds

| Metric | Good | Needs improvement | Poor ||---|---|---|---|| LCP | ≤ 2.5 s | 2.5 s – 4.0 s | > 4.0 s || INP | ≤ 200 ms | 200 ms – 500 ms | > 500 ms || CLS | ≤ 0.1 | 0.1 – 0.25 | > 0.25 |

Where the time goes

Each metric breaks down into parts, and each part has different fixes:

| Metric | Sub-part | Typical fix ||---|---|---|| LCP | Time to first byte (TTFB) | CDN, caching, faster server rendering || LCP | Resource load delay | Make the LCP image discoverable in the HTML; ; no lazy-loading above the fold || LCP | Resource load duration | Right-sized, modern image formats; fewer competing downloads || LCP | Element render delay | Less render-blocking CSS/JS; do not hide content until hydration || INP | Input delay | Other work already running on the main thread: long tasks, hydration, third-party scripts || INP | Processing duration | The event handlers themselves: less work, yield early, defer non-visual work || INP | Presentation delay | Rendering the next frame: DOM size, style and layout cost, forced layouts |

> Common pitfall: optimising a Lighthouse score. Lighthouse runs without a user, so it cannot measure INP at all. It reports Total Blocking Time as a lab proxy. A page can score 100 and still fail INP for real users who click during hydration.

Measuring in the field

The  library (maintained by the Chrome team) deals with the edge cases for you: back/forward-cache restores, prerendering, tabs opened in the background, and reporting the final CLS and INP values when the page is hidden. Write your own  code only if you have a reason to.

Chapter 2 — Reading the Performance panel

Record with the CPU throttled (a 4× slowdown is a common starting point) and with a realistic network profile. Your laptop is several times faster than a median phone. Then read the recording top-down:

1. Tracks overview — FPS, CPU activity (yellow = scripting, purple = rendering/layout, green = painting), network requests.2. Main track flame chart — the x-axis is time; the y-axis is call-stack depth (callers on top, callees below). Width is duration.3. Tasks with a red corner — long tasks, over 50 ms.4. Bottom panels — Summary, Bottom-up (where self time is spent, aggregated), Call tree (top-down total time), Event log.

Terminology

- Self time — time spent in a function's own code, excluding the functions it calls.- Total time — self time plus all callees. A function with high total but low self time is a coordinator; look underneath it.- Long task — a task that runs on the main thread for over 50 ms. Input that arrives meanwhile has to wait.- Forced (synchronous) layout — JavaScript reads layout (, ) after writing styles, so the browser must lay out immediately, inside your task. Repeating it in a loop is layout thrashing.

DevTools marks forced layouts with a purple Layout bar and a red warning, and links to the line that caused them.

> Trick: add your own spans.  and  appear in the Timings track, next to the flame chart, and are also available to RUM code through . Name them after user-visible operations, not after functions.

> Watch out: the flame chart shows the minified names unless source maps are loaded, and in development builds React, Vue and others add checks that change the profile. Profile a production build with source maps.

Chapter 3 — Long tasks and yielding

The main thread runs one task at a time (Post 01). A 300 ms task means any click during it waits up to 300 ms before its handler even starts. That wait is INP's input delay. The fix is to split work and yield between the pieces, so that the browser can handle input and paint.

is better than  because its continuation is prioritised: it resumes ahead of other queued tasks of the same priority, instead of going to the back of the queue behind third-party scripts. It is not yet Baseline, so keep the fallback.

For event handlers, give the user feedback before the heavy work:

Finding the culprit: Long Animation Frames

Long tasks tell you that the main thread was blocked. The Long Animation Frames API (LoAF, Chrome 123+) tells you which scripts did it, in the field:

Aggregated over thousands of sessions,  quickly separates "our bundle" from "the chat widget" and "the A/B testing snippet". The  attribution build attaches the relevant LoAF entries to INP reports for you.

> Common pitfall: moving work into  or a  without splitting it. A 400 ms task started from idle time is still a 400 ms task. It just blocks a different interaction.

Chapter 4 — Memory leaks in the browser

JavaScript is garbage-collected: an object is freed once nothing reachable from a root (globals, the stack, active handles) references it. A leak is therefore not "forgotten ". It is an unintended reference path from a root. Four paths cause most real leaks:

| Leak | Retaining path | Fix ||---|---|---|| Detached DOM | A removed element still referenced from JS: an array, a , a closure, a component instance | Drop references on unmount; hold elements in / if you must || Listeners and subscriptions | //an event bus holds the listener, the listener closes over a component | Remove in cleanup;  plus one  || Closures | A long-lived callback captures a large scope: the whole response, not the one field it needs | Capture only what you use; null out big locals || Unbounded caches | A module-level  keyed by request, user or URL, never evicted | LRU with a size bound, TTLs,  for object keys |

Timers ( never cleared) and global stores that keep every past page's data are variants of the same idea.

Terminology

- Shallow size — memory held by the object itself.- Retained size — memory that would be freed if this object were collected: itself plus everything only it keeps alive. Sort by this.- Retainers — the chain of references from a GC root to the object. The fix is almost always to break one link in this chain.- Distance — the number of references from the nearest root. Many objects at an unexpectedly large distance often point to a growing structure.

The three-snapshot technique

1. Load the page, do the action once to warm caches, take snapshot 1.2. Repeat the suspect action (open and close the dialog, navigate away and back) several times. Take snapshot 2.3. Repeat the same number of times again. Take snapshot 3.4. In snapshot 3, choose Objects allocated between snapshots 1 and 2. Objects created then and still alive after another round are leak candidates. Anything that belongs to a closed dialog should be gone.

Useful filters: type  in the class filter to list detached DOM trees, and open Retainers on one of them. The first entry under your own code (not framework internals) is usually the bug.

The Allocation instrumentation on timeline profile records allocations over time. Blue bars are allocations still alive at the end, grey bars were collected. Blue bars that keep appearing on every repeat of an action are the leak, and selecting one shows the allocating stack. Allocation sampling has much lower overhead and suits long sessions.

> Watch out:  in DevTools retains  while the console is open, so leaks can appear only with DevTools attached. Clear the console before a snapshot. Taking a snapshot also forces a full GC, so "memory drops after snapshot" is not proof of a fix.

> Trick: in production,  (Chromium, cross-origin isolated pages only) reports memory per frame. Sample it rarely and send it to RUM. A median that rises with session length is a leak you have not reproduced yet.

Chapter 5 — Profiling Node

Attaching a debugger

Open  for the same Performance and Memory tools as in the browser. Never bind the inspector to a public interface (): it gives anyone who can reach the port full code execution. On a remote host, use an SSH tunnel.

Profiles without a debugger

Load  and  files into DevTools (Performance and Memory panels), or turn a CPU profile into a flame graph with tools like speedscope.  sets the sampling interval in microseconds (default 1000).

> Watch out: writing a heap snapshot is synchronous and needs roughly twice the current heap size in extra memory. On a container that is close to its memory limit, the snapshot itself can get the process OOM-killed. Take snapshots on a canary instance, or with a raised limit.

Runtime signals worth graphing

How to read them together:

-  climbs across GCs and never returns — a JS leak. Take two heap snapshots some time apart and compare them.-  climbs while  stays flat — memory outside the JS heap: s, native addons, fragmentation. Heap snapshots will not show it.- Event loop delay p99 spikes — something synchronous is blocking: a large , a sync crypto or zlib call, a pathological regex. A CPU profile taken during the spike shows it.

(in MB) sets the old-generation heap limit. In containers, set it deliberately below the container's memory limit, so that V8 collects garbage harder before the kernel kills the process.

Chapter 6 — Source maps

A source map links positions in generated code to positions in the original source. The format was standardised as ECMA-426 (first edition, December 2024). The generated file points to its map with a trailing comment:

Deployment choices:

| Strategy | Browser DevTools | Error tracker | Source exposed publicly ||---|---|---|---|| Public maps with  | Yes | Yes | Yes || Hidden maps: generate, upload to the tracker, do not deploy, no comment | No | Yes | No || Maps behind auth / VPN only | For staff | Yes | No |

Most teams choose hidden maps. In Vite: . Upload the maps in CI together with the release identifier your runtime reports, so that the tracker can match an error from  to the map from the same build.

In Node,  (or ) rewrites stack traces to original TypeScript locations. It has a cost: building  becomes much more expensive, which matters in code that creates many errors. Measure before turning it on for a hot service.

> Common pitfall: the map uploaded to the tracker does not match the deployed file, for example because it came from a rebuild with a different hash. Every frame then maps to the wrong line, and nobody notices for weeks. Build once and deploy that exact artifact.

Chapter 7 — Production error monitoring and RUM

Capturing errors

Checklist for useful reports:

- Cross-origin scripts report only  unless the  has  and the CDN sends .- Tag every event with the release, route, browser and a session ID. Without the release you cannot tell a new regression from an old error.- Group errors by normalised stack (the fingerprint), not by message. Messages contain IDs and user data.- Walk  and include the chain (Post 03), after removing secrets.- Collect browser reports with the Reporting API: the  header plus  in CSP collects CSP violations, deprecation warnings and intervention reports without any script.- In Node,  is for logging, then exiting. The process state is unknown after an uncaught exception. Let the supervisor restart it.

RUM

Real-user monitoring means aggregating Chapter 1's metrics plus your own spans () from real sessions. Guidelines:

- Look at distributions, not averages: p75 for Web Vitals, and p95/p99 for your own spans. Always split by device class and connection. A median desktop user tells you nothing about a low-end Android phone.- Sample the high-volume events (e.g. 10% of sessions). Send errors and poor-rated vitals at 100%.- Send on  to  with .  is unreliable, and on mobile often never fires.- Correlate RUM with backend traces (OpenTelemetry's ), so that a slow LCP can be traced to the slow API call behind it.- Treat RUM as personal data: no raw URLs with tokens, no text from input fields, and only what your privacy notice covers.

Chapter 8 — Benchmarking without fooling yourself

Micro-benchmarks in JavaScript are unusually easy to get wrong, because a JIT compiler (Post 00) sits between your code and the CPU.

What goes wrong, and how to avoid it:

| Pitfall | What happens | Countermeasure ||---|---|---|| Dead-code elimination | Unused result → the optimiser may remove the work | Consume results (accumulate and print, or use a "black hole") || Constant folding | Constant input → computed once | Vary inputs; read them from an array built at runtime || No warm-up | You measure the interpreter and the compiler, not optimised code | Warm-up iterations; discard the first samples || Unrealistic type feedback | The benchmark feeds one shape, production feeds five → monomorphic vs megamorphic (Post 02) | Use production-like data || GC noise | A collection lands inside one sample | Many samples; report median and spread, not the best run || Timer resolution |  is coarsened to 100 µs (5 µs when cross-origin isolated) | Time batches, not single calls;  in Node || Machine noise | CPU frequency scaling, thermal throttling, other processes | Same machine, idle, several runs; compare A/B in the same process run |

Use a harness that handles most of this (, ), and  for whole commands. Most importantly, prefer macro-benchmarks: the real endpoint under realistic load, or the real interaction in a throttled Performance recording. A 10× gain on a function that takes 0.5% of the request is a 0.45% gain.

> Trick: before optimising anything, write down the metric, its current p75/p99, and the target. After the change, compare the same percentile from the same source. Anything else tends to become "it feels faster".

Practice Exercises

Exercise 1 — Classify the metrics

A product page has p75 LCP 3.1 s, p75 INP 180 ms and p75 CLS 0.27 on mobile. Rate each, and say which you would work on first and why.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

LCP 3.1 s is needs improvement (2.5–4.0 s). INP 180 ms is good (≤ 200 ms). CLS 0.27 is poor (> 0.25). Start with CLS: it is the only poor rating, and the usual causes are cheap to fix (images without dimensions, late-injected banners, web fonts swapping sizes). Then LCP, starting with its sub-parts from the attribution data.

</details>

Exercise 2 — Read the INP breakdown

RUM attribution for a poor INP shows input delay 380 ms, processing 40 ms, presentation 30 ms, on interactions during the first 5 s. What is happening, and what do you look at next?

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

The handler is fast. The interaction waited behind other main-thread work, and the time window points at start-up: hydration, bundle evaluation, or third-party scripts. Next, look at the LoAF  entries attached to those interactions (, ) to see whose code it was. Then break up or defer that work: yield during hydration, lazy-load third parties after interaction, and split large bundles.

</details>

Exercise 3 — Find the leak

A single-page app's tab grows by about 8 MB each time a user opens and closes a report dialog. Describe the exact DevTools steps to find the retaining path.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Memory panel: warm up once, take snapshot 1. Open and close the dialog 5 times, take snapshot 2. Another 5 times, take snapshot 3. In snapshot 3, view objects allocated between snapshots 1 and 2, sort by retained size, and filter the class list by . Pick a detached dialog root and read its Retainers from the top until the first frame in your own code. That is usually a listener on , a store subscription, or a cache entry. Fix it, repeat the three snapshots, and confirm that the between-snapshot set no longer contains dialog objects.

</details>

Exercise 4 — RSS vs heap

A Node image-resizing service's RSS grows from 300 MB to 1.8 GB over a day, while  stays around 120 MB. Where is the memory, and how do you confirm it?

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Outside the JS heap: / memory ( and  in ), native allocations in the image library, or allocator fragmentation. Graph  +  next to . If they track RSS, find who holds the Buffers: search a heap snapshot for /, and look for a cache of image bytes. If they do not, suspect the native library or fragmentation. Try a different allocator (e.g. jemalloc via ) and limit the library's own caches.

</details>

Exercise 5 — Fix the benchmark

Rewrite the broken  benchmark from Chapter 8 so that it measures something real.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

The inputs vary at runtime, the result goes into , which is printed, there is a warm-up phase, and the output reports a distribution from 30 samples. Even then, the number describes only this loop on this machine. Whether  matters in your app is a question for a profile of the real workload.

</details>

Exercise 6 — Source map hygiene

Your error tracker shows the correct file names but lines that are off by dozens of lines. List two likely causes.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

(1) The uploaded map belongs to a different build of the same file name, for example a rebuild after the upload, or non-deterministic output. Build once, and upload and deploy that exact artifact, keyed by release. (2) A later step changed the file after the map was generated (a CDN "optimiser", a banner injector, a second minifier) without updating the map. Turn off the post-processing or make it produce a composed map.

</details>

Cheat-sheet

| Question | Tool / rule ||---|---|| Is the page fast for users? | Field p75: LCP ≤ 2.5 s, INP ≤ 200 ms, CLS ≤ 0.1 || Collect vitals |  →  || Why is LCP slow? | TTFB / load delay / load duration / render delay || Why is INP slow? | input delay / processing / presentation || Which scripts block? |  type  || Break up work |  with a  fallback || Custom spans |  /  || Layout thrashing | Batch reads before writes || Leak hunting | Three snapshots, "allocated between 1 and 2", filter , read Retainers || Allocation source | Allocation instrumentation on timeline / allocation sampling || Node debugger |  (localhost only),  || Node CPU profile |  → load  in DevTools || Node heap snapshot | , needs about 2× heap free || Blocked event loop |  p99 || Heap flat, RSS growing | Buffers / native memory, not a JS leak || Private source maps |  + upload per release || Node stack traces in TS |  (costly for ) || Cross-origin errors |  + CORS header || Trustworthy benchmark | Warm-up, varied input, consume results, many samples, median + spread |

f:T7f43,The slicer is where almost all of your decisions actually live. The printer executes; the slicer decides. Yet most people use it as a box with a "quality" dropdown, which means the interesting settings — the ones that decide whether a part is strong, accurate, fast or pretty — are being chosen by a vendor who has never seen your part. This post opens the box: what the slicer computes, what the G-code it emits actually says, and what each of the two hundred settings is really controlling. The organising principle throughout is that a slicer setting is only worth changing if you can state the physical mechanism it acts through.

> Series map: 00 How 3D Printing Works → 01 First Print and Calibration → 02 Slicing in Depth (this post) → 03 Orientation, Supports and Design for Printing → 04 Materials → 05 Dimensional Accuracy and Tolerances → 06 CAD for Printing → 07 Strength, Failure Modes and Functional Parts.

Learning Outcome

- Describe the slicer's computation from mesh to G-code, stage by stage, and say which stage a given artefact comes from.- Read G-code fluently enough to find a layer change, a retraction, a temperature command and an extrusion, and to compute how much filament a move deposits.- Choose layer height with reference to nozzle diameter, and state precisely what layer height does and does not control.- Explain why extrusion width is decoupled from nozzle diameter, and use that decoupling deliberately.- Set perimeter count, perimeter order and seam placement from the mechanism rather than from habit.- Specify solid skins by thickness rather than by layer count.- Compare infill patterns honestly, separating what is measurable at hobby level from what is not.- Reason about speed and acceleration well enough to know when a speed setting is not being reached at all.- Use modifiers, per-region settings and the quality features (ironing, adaptive layers, fuzzy skin, Arachne) for the cases each was built for.- Maintain a profile discipline that makes a good print reproducible six months later.

Chapter 1 — What a slicer actually computes

Terminology

- Mesh — the triangle soup the slicer receives. Must be manifold (watertight, every edge shared by exactly two faces, consistent normals) for slicing to be well defined.- Layer polygon — the set of closed 2D contours produced by intersecting the mesh with a horizontal plane at one Z. Outer contours are the part; inner contours are holes.- Offset (inset) — shrinking a polygon inward by a fixed distance. Perimeter generation is repeated offsetting.- Perimeter / wall — a closed extrusion loop following the layer's contour.- Skin / solid layer — fully dense region on a top or bottom surface.- Sparse infill — the low-density interior lattice.- Toolpath — an ordered sequence of moves with extrusion amounts attached.- Seam — the point where a closed perimeter loop starts and ends.- Extrusion width — the intended width of one deposited road. A slicer parameter, not a nozzle property.

The pipeline

Two things are worth noticing about this list.

Step 3 is why mesh quality matters. A plane–mesh intersection only yields closed loops if the mesh is closed. A hole in the mesh gives an open polyline, which the slicer must guess how to close — and its guess is where the weird stray extrusion in the middle of your part comes from. Fix the model, not the slicer settings.

Step 5 is why "top layers" behaves unintuitively. Solid skin is not a property of the top face; it is a boolean result computed per layer. A shallow dome has a top surface at nearly every layer, so it gets skin over a large part of its height, and that is why gently sloped surfaces are slow and material-hungry.

The extrusion amount

For each extrusion path, the slicer computes how many millimetres of filament to feed:

The road cross-section is where slicers differ slightly. A naive model treats it as a rectangle, . A better one treats it as a rectangle with semicircular ends — the shape a squashed extrudate actually takes:

For a 0.45 mm wide, 0.20 mm tall road these differ by around 10 %. That difference is real, it is one reason two slicers produce visibly different results from the same settings, and it is exactly the kind of systematic error your flow calibration from post 01 absorbs — which is why flow is calibrated in the slicer you actually use.

Chapter 2 — Reading the G-code

G-code is a flat list of commands. There is no loop, no variable, no geometry — every move is spelled out. A 40 MB file is 40 MB of  lines.

The commands that matter

| Command | Meaning ||---|---||  /  | linear move.  nominally means "rapid, no extrusion"; most printer firmwares treat the two identically, and many slicers emit  for everything ||  | target coordinates in mm ||  | the extruder axis, in mm of filament — not mm of travel ||  | feedrate in mm per minute. Divide by 60 to get the mm/s in your slicer UI ||  /  | absolute / relative extrusion mode. Relative () is now the common choice ||  | reset the extruder position counter (used by absolute-mode slicers to avoid huge E values) ||  | set nozzle temperature and continue ||  | set nozzle temperature and wait until reached ||  /  | same pair for the bed ||  | part cooling fan speed ||  | fan off ||  | home axes ||  | set acceleration ||  | linear advance (Marlin) |

A layer change, annotated

Reading it back:

- The  and  lines are comments. The printer ignores them; the slicer's preview, and any post-processing script, rely on them entirely. Cura uses  instead — different dialect, same idea.- The layer change is a Z move surrounded by a retract/unretract pair. That pair is where seam blobs are born.- The travel move has no  word, so the extruder does not turn.-  on a 15.176 mm move means 0.5063 mm of 1.75 mm filament was fed over that distance. Working backwards gives the road cross-section, which is how you audit what your slicer is really doing.

> Trick: when a slicer setting does not appear to be doing what you expect, slice a 20 mm cube, open the G-code in a text editor and search for the feature. Five minutes of reading beats an hour of speculation, and G-code cannot lie about what it is going to do.

Chapter 3 — Layer height and extrusion width

Layer height versus nozzle diameter

The widely used rule of thumb is a practical ceiling of roughly 75–80 % of nozzle diameter — about 0.30–0.32 mm on a 0.4 mm nozzle. It is a rule of thumb, not a physical constant, and it exists for a mechanical reason worth understanding.

The flat annular face around the nozzle orifice acts as a shoe. It rides above the previous layer at the layer height and irons the emerging extrudate down into it, which is what creates the wide contact patch and the pressure that drives interdiffusion (post 00). As layer height approaches nozzle diameter, the shoe stops touching the extrudate at all: material simply falls out of the orifice as a round noodle, keys poorly to the layer below, and stacks unstably.

Caveats in both directions:

- High-flow hotends and internally-restructured nozzles change the melting side of the problem but not the shoe geometry. Some users run higher ratios successfully; verify on your own machine before designing around it.- Going very thin has its own floor. Below roughly a quarter of nozzle diameter, small Z errors and bed-mesh residuals become a large fraction of the layer, and print time grows without a proportional gain in quality.

The first-layer exception

The first layer routinely ignores the rule and runs thicker — commonly 0.2–0.3 mm even in fine profiles — for two reasons: a thicker layer swallows small bed irregularities and mesh residuals, and it produces a larger contact patch for adhesion. It is also frequently printed wider than other layers for the same reason.

What layer height does and does not control

| Layer height controls | Layer height does not control ||---|---|| Visible stepping on sloped and curved surfaces | detail in the XY plane — that is nozzle diameter and extrusion width || The Z resolution of features (a 0.15 mm step cannot exist in a 0.2 mm layer) | hole roundness or XY dimensional accuracy || Print time — roughly inversely, but not exactly | wall thickness || The number of layer interfaces per millimetre of height | whether a part is strong (interface count and interface quality pull in opposite directions; see below) || Overhang behaviour, indirectly (each layer's unsupported step scales with height) | support requirement |

On strength, resist the confident claim in either direction. Thinner layers give more interfaces but each one is smaller, cools differently and reheats more often. The published results are material- and geometry-dependent, and the effect is generally much smaller than the effect of orientation (post 03) or of wall count. Spend your attention there instead.

Extrusion width is not nozzle diameter

This surprises people, and it is the single most useful conceptual unlock in the slicer.

The width of a deposited road is set by how much material you push per unit of travel and how high the nozzle is, not by the orifice diameter. Push more, and the extrudate spreads sideways under the shoe until it is wider. Push less, and it is narrower. The orifice only limits how fast you can push it.

Usable range is roughly 100–200 % of nozzle diameter. Below about 100 %, the road becomes unreliable and the nozzle drags. Above about 200 %, the extrudate escapes past the shoe's control and the surface becomes lumpy.

How to use the decoupling deliberately:

| Setting | Typical choice | Why ||---|---|---|| First layer | wider than default | more contact patch, better adhesion, more tolerance of bed variation || External perimeter | at or slightly below nozzle diameter | narrower roads follow corners more tightly and give better dimensional control on the visible surface || Internal perimeters | above nozzle diameter (often 110–120 %) | fewer, fatter roads for the same wall thickness — faster and fewer internal interfaces || Solid infill | above nozzle diameter | fewer passes to fill an area || Sparse infill | wider still | infill is invisible; width buys speed directly || Support interface | narrower | finer surface where the part rests on it |

> Common pitfall: setting an extrusion width and forgetting that flow calibration (post 01) targets a specific width. Change your external perimeter width and your measured wall thickness changes, which looks like a flow error and is not.

Chapter 4 — Walls: count, order, seams, gap fill

Perimeter count

Wall thickness ≈ perimeter count × extrusion width. Two perimeters at 0.45 mm gives a 0.9 mm wall; four gives 1.8 mm.

Perimeter count is usually a better investment than infill density for stiffness and strength, and the reason is elementary beam mechanics: in bending, material's contribution to stiffness scales with the square of its distance from the neutral axis. The walls are the material furthest from the neutral axis. Infill in the middle of the section contributes comparatively little. Going from two walls to four on a bracket typically does more than doubling infill, and costs less time and material.

Typical practice: 2 walls for decorative parts, 3–4 for general functional parts, 5+ for parts under real load or that need to be watertight or tapped.

Perimeter order

Two orders, both defensible:

Inner first, external last (the usual default). The external perimeter is laid against already-solid inner walls, so it has something to lean on, and nothing is printed afterwards to disturb it. Generally better surface quality and better behaviour on overhanging walls.

External first. The outer wall is laid before any inner wall can push it outward, which can improve dimensional accuracy on some machines. Costs surface quality and overhang reliability.

Slicers in the PrusaSlicer lineage expose this as an "external perimeters first" toggle, and some offer a hybrid ordering. The honest guidance is that the effect size is machine-dependent: print a test part both ways, measure it with calipers and look at it, and decide for your machine. Post 05 returns to the accuracy side of this.

Seams

Every closed loop must start and stop somewhere. At that point the extruder transitions from zero pressure to steady pressure and back, and the result is a small defect — a blob, a notch, or both — stacked on every layer.

Seam placement strategies:

| Strategy | Result ||---|---|| Nearest | seam goes wherever travel is shortest; fastest, scattered, unpredictable || Aligned | seams stack into a single vertical line; ugly but predictable, and you can rotate it to the back || Rear | forced to the back of the plate || Random | scattered; hides the line at the cost of scattering small defects across the whole surface || Painted | you choose, per region, in the slicer — put it inside a corner where geometry hides it |

Mechanically, a stack of aligned seams is a line of small stress concentrations and reduced wall continuity, so on a pressure-bearing or load-bearing part, do not let the seam line up with the region of highest stress.

Reducing the seam artefact itself: tune pressure advance (post 01), and use the slicer's wipe and coasting options, which stop extruding slightly before the end of the loop and move the nozzle to smear the remainder. Some slicers additionally offer a scarf or ramped seam that spreads the transition over a Z ramp instead of a single point; where available, it is the most effective option for a visible cylindrical surface.

Gap fill

When a region is too narrow for another whole perimeter but too wide to ignore, classic perimeter generation leaves a gap and fills it with a short, thin extrusion. Gap fill is a common source of noise, vibration, blobs and stuttering motion, because it is many tiny moves with rapid flow changes.

The modern answer is variable-width extrusion (Chapter 6), which mostly eliminates the problem by widening the perimeters themselves to close the gap.

Chapter 5 — Skins and infill

The thickness-not-count rule

Solid layers are specified in most slicers as a count, which is a trap. What matters physically is total thickness of solid material.

Halving your layer height silently halves your top skin. The result is pillowing: the top surface sags into the infill voids and shows holes and bumps.

The rule: specify a thickness, then derive the count. Around 0.8–1.2 mm of top solid is a sensible general target; more if infill density is low, because thin skin has to bridge longer unsupported spans. Modern slicers have a "minimum shell thickness" setting that enforces this automatically — turn it on. Bottom skins can be thinner, because the build plate supports them.

Monotonic ordering for the top solid layer is worth enabling where available: it forces solid infill lines to be laid in a consistent direction so each new road overlaps the previous one the same way, producing a visually uniform surface instead of a patchwork.

Infill patterns, honestly

First, what is reliably measurable at hobby level and what is not.

Measurable, repeatable, and worth optimising: estimated and actual print time, material mass (weigh the part), whether the pattern self-intersects within a layer (which makes the nozzle run over its own deposits, adding noise and artefacts), and whether the pattern is 2D (identical every layer) or 3D (varies with Z, giving out-of-plane structure).

Not reliably measurable without a test rig, standardised coupons and repeated samples: the small percentage strength differences between patterns that appear in blog comparisons. These figures are usually a single specimen, in a single load direction, on a single machine, and they do not generalise. Treat them as anecdote.

| Pattern | Structure | Print speed | Material at equal density | Honest strength note ||---|---|---|---|---|| Rectilinear / lines | 2D, alternating direction per layer | fastest | lowest | no intersections within a layer; layers only touch at crossings, so poor out-of-plane contribution || Grid | 2D, both directions in the same layer | fast | low | nozzle crosses its own deposits at every intersection — can cause knocking and artefacts at high density || Triangles | 2D, three directions | moderate | moderate | good in-plane isotropy; more material at nodes || Honeycomb | 2D hexagonal | slow — many direction changes | moderate | high in-plane stiffness for its mass, paid for in time || Cubic / adaptive cubic | 3D, stacked cubes on a corner | moderate | moderate; adaptive saves a lot | contributes in all three axes; adaptive subdivides only near surfaces || Gyroid | 3D, smooth continuous non-intersecting surface | moderate | moderate | near-isotropic by construction; smooth continuous motion; the common default for functional parts, and good for flexibles because it compresses evenly || Lightning | tree-like, grows only where a top surface needs support | very fast | very low | contributes essentially nothing structurally — that is the point. For decorative parts and hollow bodies || Concentric | follows the contour | moderate | moderate | used for flexible parts, where following the wall keeps the part uniformly compliant |

Density

Strength does not scale linearly with infill density; the returns diminish, and the time cost does not. The efficient move is almost always: add walls before you add infill, for the second-moment-of-area reason in Chapter 4. Typical practice:

- Decorative: 5–15 %, or lightning.- General functional: 15–25 % with 3–4 walls.- Loaded parts: 30–50 % with 5+ walls — and read post 07 before assuming that is enough.- 100 %: rarely correct. Over-packing solid infill causes over-extrusion, dimensional growth and worse surfaces. If you need a solid part, increase walls until they meet in the middle.

Chapter 6 — Motion, and the quality features

Per-feature speeds

Slicers set a different speed for external perimeters, internal perimeters, solid infill, sparse infill, top solid, bridges, gap fill and travel. The important asymmetry:

Outer-wall speed dominates surface quality, and costs very little total time. The external perimeter is the only extrusion anyone sees; it is where ringing lands, where pressure-advance error is visible, and where corner rounding shows. It is also a small fraction of total extruded volume on most parts. Halving outer-wall speed typically adds a few percent to print time and produces a visibly better part. This is the best quality-per-minute trade in the whole slicer.

Conversely, sparse infill speed is nearly free to raise — until it hits the volumetric flow ceiling you measured in post 01.

Acceleration is the real print-time variable

On small parts the toolhead rarely reaches its commanded speed at all. The distance needed to accelerate from rest to speed  at acceleration  is:

Accelerating and decelerating therefore needs about 15.6 mm of travel. A 10 mm wall segment never sees 250 mm/s. It accelerates over half its length and decelerates over the other half, so its peak speed is  =  =  = 200 mm/s, and its average is lower still.

Consequences:

- Raising the speed setting on a part made of short segments changes nothing. Raising acceleration does.- Raising acceleration excites resonance, which is what input shaping (post 01) exists to manage. The two settings are coupled.- Jerk (Marlin's classic model), junction deviation and Klipper's square corner velocity all describe the same thing: how much speed is retained through a direction change. Raising it speeds up detailed parts and adds ringing.- Minimum layer time / "slow down for short layers" forces small cross-sections to take long enough to solidify. It is a cooling setting wearing a speed setting's clothes.

Cooling

Set from material, as measured in post 01. Briefly: PLA wants a lot, PETG a moderate amount, ABS/ASA very little in an enclosure, nylon and PC little. Cooling buys overhang and detail quality and pays in layer adhesion; that trade never goes away.

The quality features

Ironing. After the top solid layer is complete, the nozzle sweeps the surface again at very low flow, remelting and smoothing it. Produces a genuinely flat, near-glossy top face. Costs time proportional to top-surface area, and can push a bead of material to the edges if flow is too high. Only applies to flat, horizontal top surfaces.

Adaptive layer height. Layer height varies with local surface slope — thin layers where the surface is shallow (where stepping is visible), thick where it is vertical (where stepping is invisible). Excellent on organic and curved shapes, useless on prisms, and it complicates any setting that is expressed per layer. Some slicers additionally let you paint a height range by hand.

Fuzzy skin. The outer wall is deliberately jittered in XY by a small random amount, giving a matte, textured surface. It hides layer lines, hides seams, and improves grip on handles. Costs a little time and a little dimensional precision on the fuzzed surface — never fuzz a mating face. Better slicers let you paint it onto selected surfaces only.

Variable-width extrusion (Arachne). The perimeter generator that, instead of laying fixed-width loops and dumping the remainder into gap fill, varies each loop's width to fill the available space exactly. Thin walls, tapering ribs, embossed text and small features all improve substantially. It originated in the Cura engine and became the default perimeter generator in PrusaSlicer from version 2.5; OrcaSlicer and Bambu Studio, both of PrusaSlicer lineage, carry it too. The classic fixed-width generator remains selectable and is occasionally preferred when you want perfectly uniform road width for its own sake.

Modifiers and per-region settings

The most under-used capability in every modern slicer. You are not limited to one setting per part.

- Per-object settings — different infill, walls or supports for each object on the plate.- Height ranges — different settings for a Z band, e.g. more solid layers around a threaded region.- Modifier meshes — drop a box or cylinder into the scene and any setting inside its volume is overridden. The canonical use is 100 % infill only inside a screw boss, or extra walls only around a bearing seat.- Negative volumes — subtract geometry at slice time without touching the CAD model. Useful for a quick clearance test.- Support blockers and enforcers — force supports on or off in a region rather than fighting the global threshold.- Painted seams and painted supports — direct manual control on the model surface.

> Trick: a modifier mesh is the correct answer almost every time you are tempted to raise a global setting for the sake of one feature. Raising global infill from 20 % to 60 % because one boss needs to be solid costs you the whole part's print time; a modifier box costs you the boss.

Brims, rafts and skirts

| Feature | What it is | Use when ||---|---|---|| Skirt | one or more loops around the part, not touching it | always — it purges the nozzle and gives you a free look at the first layer before the part starts || Brim | a flat collar attached to the part's first layer | small footprints, tall thin parts, warp-prone materials, sharp corners that lift. Costs cleanup and can mar the bottom edge || Raft | a full printed platform under the part | a genuinely bad bed surface, materials that will not adhere directly, or parts whose footprint is nearly nothing. Costs material, time and the bottom-face finish |

"Mouse ear" brims — small discs added only at the corners that lift — give most of the benefit of a full brim with a fraction of the cleanup, and are available as a brim type or can be added as small modifier objects.

Chapter 7 — Profiles, slicers, and estimates

Profile discipline

The rule: start from the vendor profile, change one variable, keep notes.

1. Begin from the printer manufacturer's profile for your machine and a material profile for your filament. These encode machine limits you may not know about.2. Change one thing. Print the test that isolates it. Record the result.3. Save as a new named profile whose name says what changed —  beats .4. Export the project as a 3MF with settings embedded for anything you might reprint. A bare STL plus a memory of what you did is not reproducible; a project file is.5. Keep the same notebook you started in post 01. Slicer changes and machine changes belong in the same log because they interact.

The slicer landscape, by capability

All four of the mainstream desktop slicers below share substantial code lineage, so most setting names transfer between them.

| Slicer | Lineage | Notable capabilities ||---|---|---|| PrusaSlicer | descends from Slic3r; open source | strong documentation; Arachne default from 2.5; organic supports from 2.6; modifier meshes, height ranges, negative volumes; wide community printer profiles || OrcaSlicer | fork in the PrusaSlicer/Bambu Studio lineage; open source | built-in guided calibration suite (flow, pressure advance, temperature, maximum volumetric speed, retraction); broad printer support; fast adoption of new features || Cura | Ultimaker; open source | very large printer profile library; plugin marketplace; the engine where tree supports, lightning infill and the Arachne generator originated || Bambu Studio | PrusaSlicer lineage | tight integration with its own hardware, multi-material and multi-plate workflow |

Choose on the capability you need — the calibration suite, the profile library, the plugin, the hardware integration — rather than on identity. Being able to read a setting in one and find it in another is a more valuable skill than loyalty to any of them.

Print time and material cost estimates

Print time. The slicer estimates by simulating its own motion model: every move, with acceleration and jerk applied. It is a good model, and the error comes from the places where the printer does not behave like the model — firmware acceleration limits that clamp the slicer's requests, input shaping smoothing that changes effective acceleration, per-move minimum times, and heating and homing at the start.

The right response is calibration rather than complaint:

If the ratio is wildly different for small versus large parts, the discrepancy is in acceleration handling, not in the estimator.

Material. The slicer reports extruded filament length and usually a mass. Mass comes from , and the density value lives in the filament profile. For unfilled PLA and PETG the default is usually close; for carbon- or glass-filled, metal-filled, wood-filled or foaming materials it can be significantly off. Verify once per material by weighing a printed part on a kitchen scale and comparing.

Cost. The naive figure is . The realistic figure adds electricity, amortised machine and consumable cost (nozzles, plates, belts), and — the term everyone omits — a failure allowance. If one print in eight fails at 70 % completion, your effective material cost is meaningfully higher than the slicer's number, and your effective time cost is much higher.

10:T3c63,Sooner or later every developer types  and gets . This is not a bug in Python or JavaScript. It is how almost every computer stores fractional numbers: in binary, with a fixed number of bits. This post opens up that format (IEEE 754 binary64), shows exactly where the error comes from, and gives you the practical rules: how to compare floats, where integers stop being exact, how to add many numbers, how to store money, which rounding rule your language uses, and why 3D scenes jitter far from the origin.

> Series map: 01 Text Encoding → … → 07 HTTPS → 08 Floating Point (this post) → 09 Versions & Dependencies → … → 24 Desk Health. Part B · Everyday skills.

Learning Outcome

- Explain why  has no exact binary form, and read the bits of a  in Python.- Describe the IEEE 754 binary64 layout: 1 sign bit, 11 exponent bits, 52 fraction bits.- Compare floats with a tolerance (), and know when absolute vs relative tolerance fits.- Know the 2^53 limit for exact integers, and handle ,  and infinities without surprises.- Reduce summation error with  or Kahan summation.- Store money as integer minor units or a decimal type, and pick a rounding mode on purpose.- Explain float32 precision loss on GPUs and the Three.js depth-buffer options.

Chapter 1 — The famous result

JavaScript prints the same , and  is . Both languages use the same number format, so both give the same answer.

Why 0.1 cannot be stored exactly

In decimal,  never ends: . The reason is that 3 does not divide any power of 10. Binary has the same problem with any fraction whose denominator has a factor other than 2. And  has a 5, so in binary it repeats forever:

A float keeps only 53 significant bits, so the computer stores the nearest representable number. You can see that number exactly:

So  is really a bit more than 0.1,  is a bit more than 0.2, and their sum lands on a different double than the one nearest to 0.3.

Why does  show  then? Python and JavaScript print the shortest decimal string that reads back to the same double.  is shorter than the long exact value and maps to the same bits, so it is chosen. The stored value did not change; only the printout is short.

Fractions whose denominator is a power of two are exact: , , . Therefore  is .

Chapter 2 — The IEEE 754 binary64 layout

Python , JavaScript , Java/C/C++  and MongoDB  all use binary64 from IEEE 754 (current edition: IEEE 754-2019). It has 64 bits:

For normal numbers the value is:

$$x = (-1)^{s} \times 1.f2 \times 2^{\,e - 1023}$$

- sign : 0 is positive, 1 is negative.- exponent : stored with a bias of 1023, so stored 1023 means 2^0.- fraction : the bits after the binary point. The leading  is implied and not stored, so you get 53 significant bits from 52 stored ones.

Look at real bits:

For  the exponent bits are  = 1019, and 1019 − 1023 = −4. The fraction is the repeating  pattern, and the last bits  show it was rounded up. So 0.1 is stored as about 1.6 × 2^−4, just a little high.

Special exponent values

| Exponent bits | Fraction | Meaning ||---------------|----------|---------|| all 0 | 0 |  or  || all 0 | not 0 | Subnormal: tiny numbers near zero, with less precision || all 1 | 0 |  or  || all 1 | not 0 | NaN (not a number) |

in Python lists the limits:  is , the smallest normal positive value  is ,  is 53 bits, and  is 15 (decimal digits that always survive a round trip). Rule of thumb: a double carries about 15 to 17 significant decimal digits.

Terminology

| Term | Meaning ||------|---------|| Significand (mantissa) | The  part: the significant bits. || Exponent / bias | Power of two, stored as . || ULP | "Unit in the last place": the gap between a float and the next one up. || Machine epsilon | ULP of 1.0:  ≈ . || Round-to-nearest-even | Default IEEE rounding for every arithmetic result. |

Chapter 4 — Integers past 2^53

A double has 53 significant bits, so every integer up to 2^53 = 9,007,199,254,740,992 is exact. Past that, the gap between doubles is 2, then 4, and so on:

Python  has arbitrary precision, so this only bites when an integer passes through  (division with , , JSON parsers, NumPy, pandas). In JavaScript every plain number is a double, so it bites all the time:

> Common pitfall: 64-bit database IDs sent as JSON numbers. A browser silently changes  to , and then you update the wrong record. Send large IDs as strings, and convert them to  only if you must do arithmetic.

Chapter 7 — Storing money

Rule: never store or calculate money in binary floating point. Amounts like 19.99 cannot be stored exactly, and the errors show up as cents that do not add up. There are two safe designs.

Option A — integer minor units

Store the amount as an integer count of the smallest unit: 19.99 →  cents. Integers are exact, and addition is exact.

- Store the currency next to the amount. Currencies have different minor units under ISO 4217: JPY has 0 decimal places, USD and EUR have 2, and KWD and BHD have 3.- Division needs a remainder policy. Splitting 100.00 three ways must still add up to 100.00:

Option B — decimal types

A decimal type stores base-10 digits, so  is exact.

Python  — always construct from strings (or integers), never from floats:

Notice the last line: rounding tax per line gives 5.88, rounding once on the total gives 5.87. Both are "correct". Your business rules (or tax law) must say which one to use, and the code must follow it every time.

MongoDB  — IEEE 754 decimal128, with 34 decimal digits of precision. MongoDB's "Model Monetary Data" guide recommends it, and its mongosh examples build values from strings:

The same guide also describes the integer "scale factor" option (store 9.99 as  with a scale of 1000). Types are introduced in MongoDB Course 00 · Foundations.

SQL  —  total digits,  digits after the point, so  holds up to 9,999,999,999.99. Add a  column for the ISO 4217 code. PostgreSQL's docs say  "is especially recommended for storing monetary amounts".

JavaScript has no built-in decimal type. Keep integer cents (use  for very large totals) or use a well-maintained decimal library. Format only for display with :

Chapter 8 — Rounding modes

"Round 2.5" has more than one right answer. It depends on the rounding mode:

| Mode (Python  name) | 2.5 | −2.5 | 2.4 | Typical use ||------------------------------|-----|------|-----|-------------||  ("banker's") | 2 | −2 | 2 | IEEE default, Python ,  default ||  (ties away from zero) | 3 | −3 | 2 | School rounding, many invoices, SQL  ||  | 2 | −2 | 2 | Rare ||  (away from zero) | 3 | −3 | 3 | "Never undercharge" rules ||  (toward zero, truncate) | 2 | −2 | 2 | Truncating fees ||  /  | 3 / 2 | −2 / −3 | 3 / 2 | Toward +∞ / −∞ |

Half-to-even sends ties up half the time and down half the time, so a long column of rounded numbers does not drift upward. That is why it is the IEEE default.

What your language actually does

Python's docs confirm: ties go "toward the even choice". And  gives , which the docs note "is not a bug": the stored double is , which is below the tie. When you need a specific mode, use  built from a string:

JavaScript is different again:

PostgreSQL rounds  ties away from zero, but  ties to even ("on most machines", says its manual):  is 3,  is 2. The same  call can give different answers depending on the column type.

Chapter 9 — Formatting is not the stored value

Formatting with  or  changes the text, not the number. Some rules:

- Round on purpose, at boundaries: when you store, when you send to an API, when you show to a user. Do not round in the middle of a calculation "to clean it up".- Never compare formatted strings, and never parse your own display output back into numbers. Keep the raw value.- Formatting for humans is locale-dependent ( vs ). Use  or Python's  / Babel for display, and a fixed machine format () in files and APIs.

Chapter 10 — float32 on GPUs

GPUs mostly compute in binary32 ("float32"): 1 sign bit, 8 exponent bits, 23 fraction bits, so 24 significant bits and about 7 decimal digits. WebGL vertex buffers are usually . The gap between neighbouring values grows fast:

If one unit is one metre, an object 6,371 km from the origin (Earth's radius) can only sit on a 0.5 m grid in float32. The camera and models snap between grid points, and you see jitter. The usual fixes:

- Keep the action near the origin. Move the world instead of the camera ("floating origin"), or rebase coordinates when the camera travels far.- Camera-relative rendering. Compute positions relative to the camera in float64 on the CPU (JavaScript numbers are float64), then send small float32 offsets to the GPU.

Depth precision and z-fighting

The depth buffer has the same problem in the depth direction. With a perspective camera most depth precision sits near the near plane. Far away, two nearly touching surfaces get the same depth value and flicker through each other: z-fighting. In Three.js:

The Three.js docs say  "may be necessary" for "huge differences in scale". They also note that it uses , which turns off the early fragment test and can cost performance. The docs list a newer  option too. It needs the  extension, and the docs call it faster and more accurate than the logarithmic buffer. The / rule of thumb is covered in Three.js Course 02 · Cameras, Controls, and Coordinate Systems.
