11:T7443,Much of analysis builds functions as limits: $e^x$ as a power series, $\sin x$ as a Taylor series, the Weierstrass function of post 03 as a sum of cosines, solutions of differential equations as limits of approximations. The question is always the same: which properties survive the limit? Continuity, integrals and derivatives can all be lost under the obvious "point by point" notion of convergence. This post introduces the stronger notion that repairs most of this, uniform convergence, proves exactly what it preserves (continuity and integrals) and what it does not (derivatives, without an extra hypothesis), and then applies it to power series: radius of convergence, term-by-term calculus, Abel's theorem, and the difference between a Taylor series and the function it came from. It closes the core course with the Weierstrass approximation theorem and a map of where analysis goes next.

> Series map: 00 The Real Numbers & Completeness → 01 Sequences & Series → 02 Topology of ℝ & Continuity → 03 Differentiation & Taylor's Theorem → 04 The Riemann Integral → 05 Uniform Convergence & Power Series (this post).

Learning Outcome

- Define pointwise and uniform convergence of a sequence of functions, and tell them apart with $x^n$ on $[0, 1]$.- Use the sup-norm test and the Cauchy criterion for uniform convergence.- Prove that a uniform limit of continuous functions is continuous (the $\varepsilon/3$ argument).- Prove that integrals commute with uniform limits, and show with an example that pointwise limits break this.- State the theorem on limits of derivatives and give the counterexample that shows why it needs uniform convergence of the derivatives.- State and prove the Weierstrass M-test and use it on series of functions.- Compute the radius of convergence with the Cauchy–Hadamard formula and the ratio test, and check the endpoints.- Prove that a power series converges uniformly on every closed interval inside its radius, and differentiate and integrate it term by term.- State Abel's theorem and use it to show $1 - \frac12 + \frac13 - \dots = \ln 2$.- Explain why a smooth function need not equal its Taylor series, and state the Weierstrass approximation theorem with the Bernstein-polynomial idea.

Chapter 1 — Pointwise versus uniform convergence

Terminology

- Sequence of functions — functions $fn : A \to \mathbb{R}$, $n = 1, 2, \dots$, all on the same domain $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$.- Pointwise convergence — $fn \to f$ pointwise on $A$ if for each fixed $x \in A$, the number sequence $fn(x)$ converges to $f(x)$. In quantifiers: for every $x \in A$ and every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $N$ (which may depend on both $x$ and $\varepsilon$) with $\lvert fn(x) - f(x)\rvert < \varepsilon$ for $n \geq N$.- Uniform convergence — $fn \to f$ uniformly on $A$ if for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $N$ (depending on $\varepsilon$ only) with $\lvert fn(x) - f(x)\rvert < \varepsilon$ for all $n \geq N$ and all $x \in A$.- Sup norm — for a bounded $g : A \to \mathbb{R}$, $\lVert g\rVert\infty = \sup{x \in A}\lvert g(x)\rvert$.- Epsilon-tube — the band of width $\varepsilon$ on each side of the graph of $f$. Uniform convergence says: from some $N$ on, the whole graph of every $fn$ lies inside the tube.

The only difference between the two definitions is the order of "there is $N$" and "for all $x$". Uniform convergence implies pointwise convergence; the converse fails.

Sup-norm test. $fn \to f$ uniformly on $A$ if and only if $\lVert fn - f\rVert\infty \to 0$ (where $\lVert fn - f\rVert\infty$ is finite for large $n$). This is the definition rewritten: "$\lvert fn(x) - f(x)\rvert < \varepsilon$ for all $x$" gives $\sup \leq \varepsilon$, and $\sup < \varepsilon$ gives it for all $x$.

The key example: x to the n on [0, 1]

Let $fn(x) = x^n$ on $[0, 1]$. For $0 \leq x < 1$, $x^n \to 0$ (post 01); at $x = 1$, $1^n = 1$. So the pointwise limit is

$$f(x) = \begin{cases} 0 & 0 \leq x < 1, \\ 1 & x = 1. \end{cases}$$

Every $fn$ is continuous, and the limit is not. The convergence is not uniform: the point $xn = (1/2)^{1/n}$ lies in $[0, 1)$ and $fn(xn) - f(xn) = \frac12$, so $\lVert fn - f\rVert\infty \geq \frac12$ for every $n$ (in fact the sup over $[0,1)$ is $1$). The "bad point" slides towards $1$:

| $n$ | 1 | 2 | 5 | 10 | 50 ||---|---|---|---|---|---|| $xn = (1/2)^{1/n}$ | $0.5$ | $0.7071$ | $0.8706$ | $0.9330$ | $0.9862$ |

For each fixed $x < 1$ you eventually get close to $0$, but no single $N$ works for all $x$ at once.

On a smaller domain the problem disappears. On $[0, \frac12]$, $\lVert x^n - 0\rVert\infty = (\frac12)^n \to 0$, so $x^n \to 0$ uniformly on $[0, \frac12]$, and indeed on $[0, r]$ for any $r < 1$.

Damping the bad point. Multiply by $(1 - x)$: $kn(x) = x^n(1 - x)$ on $[0, 1]$. The pointwise limit is $0$ everywhere (including $x = 1$, where $kn(1) = 0$). Setting $kn'(x) = x^{n-1}\bigl(n - (n+1)x\bigr) = 0$ gives the maximum at $x = \frac{n}{n+1}$, so

$$\lVert kn\rVert\infty = \left(\frac{n}{n+1}\right)^n \frac{1}{n+1} < \frac{1}{n+1} \to 0.$$

So $kn \to 0$ uniformly on all of $[0, 1]$. For example, $\lVert k{10}\rVert\infty = \frac{10^{10}}{11^{11}} \approx 0.0350$. The factor $(1 - x)$ kills the values near $1$ exactly where $x^n$ misbehaved.

A uniform example: smoothing the corner of the absolute value

Let $gn(x) = \sqrt{x^2 + 1/n^2}$ on $\mathbb{R}$. With $h = 1/n$,

$$0 \leq \sqrt{x^2 + h^2} - \lvert x\rvert = \frac{h^2}{\sqrt{x^2 + h^2} + \lvert x\rvert} \leq \frac{h^2}{h} = h,$$

with equality at $x = 0$. So $\lVert gn - \lvert x\rvert\rVert\infty = \frac1n \to 0$: uniform convergence to $\lvert x\rvert$ on all of $\mathbb{R}$. With $\varepsilon = 0.25$, every $gn$ with $n \geq 5$ lies inside the $\varepsilon$-tube around $\lvert x\rvert$ (its largest error is $\frac15 = 0.2$), while $g2$ (error $0.5$) does not.

Every $gn$ is smooth, and the limit $\lvert x\rvert$ has a corner. Uniform convergence preserved continuity but not differentiability. Chapter 2 explains both facts.

The Cauchy criterion for uniform convergence

Theorem. A sequence $(fn)$ converges uniformly on $A$ if and only if for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $N$ with

$$\lvert fn(x) - fm(x)\rvert < \varepsilon \quad \text{for all } m, n \geq N \text{ and all } x \in A.$$

Proof. ($\Rightarrow$) If $\lvert fn - f\rvert < \varepsilon/2$ everywhere for $n \geq N$, then $\lvert fn - fm\rvert \leq \lvert fn - f\rvert + \lvert f - fm\rvert < \varepsilon$.

($\Leftarrow$) For each fixed $x$, $(fn(x))$ is a Cauchy sequence of reals, so it converges (completeness, post 01); call the limit $f(x)$. Given $\varepsilon$, take $N$ from the hypothesis. For $n \geq N$ and any $x$, let $m \to \infty$ in $\lvert fn(x) - fm(x)\rvert < \varepsilon$ to get $\lvert fn(x) - f(x)\rvert \leq \varepsilon$. The same $N$ works for every $x$, so the convergence is uniform. $\blacksquare$

The value of the criterion: it proves uniform convergence without knowing the limit in advance. The M-test in Chapter 3 is built on it.

Chapter 2 — What uniform convergence preserves

Continuity

Theorem. If each $fn$ is continuous at $c \in A$ and $fn \to f$ uniformly on $A$, then $f$ is continuous at $c$.

Proof (the $\varepsilon/3$ argument). Let $\varepsilon > 0$. Choose $N$ with $\lvert fN(x) - f(x)\rvert < \varepsilon/3$ for all $x \in A$. Since $fN$ is continuous at $c$, choose $\delta$ with $\lvert fN(x) - fN(c)\rvert < \varepsilon/3$ for $\lvert x - c\rvert < \delta$. Then for such $x$,

$$\lvert f(x) - f(c)\rvert \leq \lvert f(x) - fN(x)\rvert + \lvert fN(x) - fN(c)\rvert + \lvert fN(c) - f(c)\rvert < \frac\varepsilon3 + \frac\varepsilon3 + \frac\varepsilon3 = \varepsilon. \qquad \blacksquare$$

The first and third terms need the same $N$ at two different points, $x$ and $c$. That is exactly what uniformity supplies and pointwise convergence does not. The theorem also gives a quick proof that $x^n$ does not converge uniformly on $[0, 1]$: the limit is discontinuous.

A partial converse (Dini's theorem). If $K$ is compact, each $fn$ is continuous on $K$, $fn(x) \geq f{n+1}(x)$ for all $x$ and $n$, and $fn \to f$ pointwise with $f$ continuous, then the convergence is uniform. (Rudin, Theorem 7.13.) All four hypotheses are needed; $x^n$ on $[0, 1]$ fails only "the limit is continuous".

Integrals

Theorem. If each $fn$ is Riemann integrable on $[a, b]$ and $fn \to f$ uniformly on $[a, b]$, then $f$ is integrable and

$$\inta^b f = \lim{n \to \infty} \inta^b fn.$$

Proof. Let $\varepsilonn = \lVert fn - f\rVert\infty$, which tends to $0$. Then $fn - \varepsilonn \leq f \leq fn + \varepsilonn$ on $[a, b]$ (so $f$ is bounded). Upper and lower sums respect these inequalities, so the upper and lower integrals of $f$ (post 04) satisfy

$$\inta^b fn - \varepsilonn(b - a) \leq \underline{\inta^b} f \leq \overline{\inta^b} f \leq \inta^b fn + \varepsilonn(b - a).$$

The gap between the upper and lower integrals is at most $2\varepsilonn(b - a) \to 0$, so they are equal and $f$ is integrable. The same inequalities give $\left\lvert \inta^b f - \inta^b fn\right\rvert \leq \varepsilonn(b - a) \to 0$. $\blacksquare$

Pointwise convergence is not enough. Let $fn(x) = n x (1 - x^2)^n$ on $[0, 1]$. For $0 < x < 1$, $fn(x) \to 0$ (a geometric factor beats the factor $n$), and $fn(0) = fn(1) = 0$. So $fn \to 0$ pointwise. But

$$\int0^1 n x (1 - x^2)^n\,dx = \left[-\frac{n}{2(n+1)}(1 - x^2)^{n+1}\right]0^1 = \frac{n}{2(n+1)} \to \frac12 \neq 0 = \int0^1 0\,dx.$$

The convergence is far from uniform: the maximum of $fn$, at $x = 1/\sqrt{2n + 1}$, grows without bound (about $1.34$ for $n = 10$ and $4.28$ for $n = 100$). A travelling spike keeps area $\approx \frac12$ while its base shrinks.

Derivatives need more

Uniform convergence of $fn$ says nothing about $fn'$. Two warnings:

- $gn(x) = \sqrt{x^2 + 1/n^2} \to \lvert x\rvert$ uniformly (Chapter 1): smooth functions, non-differentiable limit.- $hn(x) = \frac{\sin(n^2 x)}{n} \to 0$ uniformly on $\mathbb{R}$ (the sup norm is $\frac1n$), but $hn'(x) = n\cos(n^2 x)$, and $hn'(0) = n \to \infty$. The derivatives do not converge to the derivative of the limit, which is $0$.

The fix is to assume uniform convergence of the derivatives.

Theorem (limits of derivatives). Let each $fn$ be differentiable on $[a, b]$. Suppose $(fn(x0))$ converges for some $x0 \in [a, b]$, and $(fn')$ converges uniformly on $[a, b]$. Then $(fn)$ converges uniformly on $[a, b]$ to a function $f$, and $f'(x) = \lim{n \to \infty} fn'(x)$ for every $x \in [a, b]$. (Rudin, Theorem 7.17.)

Idea of proof (with the extra assumption that each $fn'$ is continuous). By FTC 2 (post 04), $fn(x) = fn(x0) + \int{x0}^x fn'$. The integrals converge uniformly by the integral theorem above, so $fn \to f(x) = f(x0) + \int{x0}^x g$ with $g = \lim fn'$, which is continuous as a uniform limit. FTC 1 gives $f' = g$. The general proof uses the Mean Value Theorem instead of integrals.

> Watch out: "uniformly" in this theorem is about the derivatives. Uniform convergence of $fn$ alone never licenses swapping $\lim$ and $\frac{d}{dx}$.

Chapter 3 — Series of functions and the Weierstrass M-test

A series of functions $\sum{k=1}^{\infty} uk(x)$ converges pointwise or uniformly on $A$ when its partial sums $Sn(x) = \sum{k=1}^{n} uk(x)$ do. The three theorems of Chapter 2 become:

| If the series converges uniformly on the interval and | then ||---|---|| each $uk$ is continuous | the sum is continuous || each $uk$ is integrable on $[a, b]$ | $\int \sum uk = \sum \int uk$ (integrate term by term) || each $uk$ is differentiable, $\sum uk'$ converges uniformly, $\sum uk(x0)$ converges | $(\sum uk)' = \sum uk'$ (differentiate term by term) |

Theorem (Weierstrass M-test). Suppose $\lvert uk(x)\rvert \leq Mk$ for all $x \in A$ and all $k$, and $\sum Mk$ converges. Then $\sum uk$ converges uniformly (and absolutely) on $A$.

Proof. For $n > m$ and every $x \in A$,

$$\lvert Sn(x) - Sm(x)\rvert = \left\lvert \sum{k=m+1}^{n} uk(x)\right\rvert \leq \sum{k=m+1}^{n} Mk.$$

The right side does not depend on $x$, and it is small for large $m$ because $\sum Mk$ converges (Cauchy criterion for number series, post 01). So the partial sums satisfy the uniform Cauchy criterion of Chapter 1. $\blacksquare$

Worked example. $F(x) = \sum{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{\sin(kx)}{k^2}$. Take $Mk = \frac{1}{k^2}$; $\sum \frac1{k^2}$ converges (its value is $\frac{\pi^2}{6}$). So the series converges uniformly on $\mathbb{R}$ and $F$ is continuous. Term-by-term differentiation would give $\sum \frac{\cos(kx)}{k}$, which diverges at $x = 0$ (harmonic series). So the M-test gives continuity, and says nothing about differentiability.

The same test proves the continuity of the Weierstrass function in post 03: $\lvert a^n\cos(b^n\pi x)\rvert \leq a^n$ with $\sum a^n$ a convergent geometric series.

> Common pitfall: the M-test is sufficient, not necessary. $\sum{k \geq 1} \frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k}$ (constant functions) converges uniformly, trivially, but no summable $Mk \geq \frac1k$ exists.

Chapter 4 — Power series

Terminology

- Power series about $x0$ — a series $\sum{k=0}^{\infty} ak (x - x0)^k$ with real coefficients $ak$. Below, $x0 = 0$ for simplicity.- Radius of convergence — the number $R \in [0, \infty]$ such that the series converges absolutely for $\lvert x\rvert < R$ and diverges for $\lvert x\rvert > R$. At $x = \pm R$ anything can happen.- Limit superior — $\limsup{k} bk = \lim{n \to \infty} \sup{k \geq n} bk$, which always exists in $[-\infty, \infty]$ (monotone convergence, post 01).

Radius of convergence

Theorem (Cauchy–Hadamard). Let $\beta = \limsup{k \to \infty} \lvert ak\rvert^{1/k}$. Then the radius of convergence is $R = 1/\beta$ (with $R = \infty$ if $\beta = 0$ and $R = 0$ if $\beta = \infty$).

Proof. Apply the root test (post 01) to $\sum \lvert ak x^k\rvert$: $\limsup \lvert ak x^k\rvert^{1/k} = \beta\lvert x\rvert$. If $\beta\lvert x\rvert < 1$ the series converges absolutely; if $\beta\lvert x\rvert > 1$ the terms do not tend to $0$, so it diverges. $\blacksquare$

Ratio formula. If $\lim \left\lvert \frac{ak}{a{k+1}}\right\rvert$ exists (in $[0, \infty]$), it equals $R$. When it applies it is easier; when it does not, the root formula still works.

| Series | $R$ | At $x = R$ | At $x = -R$ ||---|---|---|---|| $\sum x^k$ | $1$ | diverges | diverges || $\sum x^k / k$ (from $k = 1$) | $1$ | diverges (harmonic) | converges (alternating) || $\sum x^k / k^2$ (from $k = 1$) | $1$ | converges | converges || $\sum x^k / k!$ | $\infty$ | — | — || $\sum k!\,x^k$ | $0$ | — | — |

The first three show that all endpoint behaviours occur with the same radius.

When the ratio test fails. Let $ak = 2^k$ for even $k$ and $ak = 1$ for odd $k$. The ratios $\lvert ak / a{k+1}\rvert$ alternate between values tending to $0$ and to $\infty$, so they have no limit. But $\lvert ak\rvert^{1/k}$ is $2$ for even $k$ and $1$ for odd $k$, so $\limsup = 2$ and $R = \frac12$.

Uniform convergence inside the radius

Theorem. If $0 < r < R$, the power series converges uniformly on $[-r, r]$. Consequently it converges uniformly on every compact subset of $(-R, R)$, and its sum is continuous on $(-R, R)$.

Proof. Since $r < R$, the series converges absolutely at $x = r$, so $\sum \lvert ak\rvert r^k$ converges. On $[-r, r]$, $\lvert ak x^k\rvert \leq \lvert ak\rvert r^k = Mk$. Apply the M-test. A compact $K \subset (-R, R)$ is closed and bounded, so its max of $\lvert x\rvert$ is some $r < R$ and $K \subseteq [-r, r]$. Every $x \in (-R, R)$ lies in some $[-r, r]$, and continuity is local. $\blacksquare$

> Watch out: the convergence need not be uniform on the whole open interval. For $\sum x^k$ on $(-1, 1)$ the tail after $x^n$ is $\sum{k > n} x^k = \frac{x^{n+1}}{1 - x}$, which is unbounded as $x \to 1^-$ for every $n$.

Term-by-term differentiation and integration

Theorem. Let $f(x) = \sum{k=0}^{\infty} ak x^k$ with radius $R > 0$. Then on $(-R, R)$:

$$f'(x) = \sum{k=1}^{\infty} k\,ak x^{k-1}, \qquad \int0^x f(t)\,dt = \sum{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{ak}{k+1} x^{k+1},$$

and both new series also have radius $R$. Hence $f$ is infinitely differentiable on $(-R, R)$, and $ak = \frac{f^{(k)}(0)}{k!}$: a power series is the Taylor series of its own sum.

Proof sketch. Since $k^{1/k} \to 1$, $\limsup (k\lvert ak\rvert)^{1/k} = \limsup \lvert ak\rvert^{1/k}$, so the differentiated series has the same radius (similarly for the integrated one). On any $[-r, r]$ with $r < R$ the differentiated series converges uniformly, so the derivative theorem (Chapter 2) applies. Integration follows from the integral theorem. Repeating gives all derivatives, and evaluating the $k$-th derivative at $0$ leaves only $k!\,ak$. $\blacksquare$

Worked example 1. From $\frac{1}{1 - x} = \sum{k \geq 0} x^k$ on $(-1, 1)$, differentiate: $\frac{1}{(1 - x)^2} = \sum{k \geq 1} k x^{k-1}$. Multiply by $x$ and put $x = \frac12$:

$$\sum{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{k}{2^k} = \frac{1/2}{(1 - 1/2)^2} = 2.$$

Worked example 2. From $\frac{1}{1 + t} = \sum{k \geq 0} (-1)^k t^k$ on $(-1, 1)$, integrate from $0$ to $x$:

$$\ln(1 + x) = x - \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^3}{3} - \dots = \sum{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k}x^k, \qquad \lvert x\rvert < 1.$$

Worked example 3. $E(x) = \sum{k \geq 0} \frac{x^k}{k!}$ has $R = \infty$. Differentiating term by term gives $E' = E$, with $E(0) = 1$. So $E(x) = e^x$ (the function $E(x)e^{-x}$ has zero derivative, hence is constant, by post 03).

Abel's theorem at the edge

The formula for $\ln(1 + x)$ was proved for $\lvert x\rvert < 1$. At $x = 1$ the series $1 - \frac12 + \frac13 - \dots$ converges (alternating series test, post 01), but does it converge to $\ln 2$?

Theorem (Abel). Suppose $\sum{k=0}^{\infty} ak$ converges. Then $f(x) = \sum ak x^k$ converges for $\lvert x\rvert < 1$ and

$$\lim{x \to 1^-} f(x) = \sum{k=0}^{\infty} ak.$$

Proof. Let $s = \sum ak$. Replacing $a0$ by $a0 - s$ changes $f$ by the constant $-s$ and makes the sum $0$, so assume $s = 0$. Let $sn = a0 + \dots + an$; then $sn \to 0$, so $(sn)$ is bounded and $\sum sk x^k$ converges for $\lvert x\rvert < 1$. Summation by parts ($ak = sk - s{k-1}$) gives, for $0 < x < 1$,

$$f(x) = \sum{k=0}^{\infty} ak x^k = (1 - x)\sum{k=0}^{\infty} sk x^k.$$

Given $\varepsilon > 0$, choose $N$ with $\lvert sk\rvert < \varepsilon$ for $k > N$. Then

$$\lvert f(x)\rvert \leq (1 - x)\sum{k=0}^{N}\lvert sk\rvert + \varepsilon(1 - x)\sum{k > N} x^k \leq (1 - x)\sum{k=0}^{N}\lvert sk\rvert + \varepsilon.$$

The first term tends to $0$ as $x \to 1^-$, so $\lvert f(x)\rvert < 2\varepsilon$ for $x$ close enough to $1$. Hence $f(x) \to 0 = s$. $\blacksquare$

Apply it to $ak = \frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k}$: the left side is $\lim{x \to 1^-}\ln(1 + x) = \ln 2$ by continuity of $\ln$. So

$$1 - \frac12 + \frac13 - \frac14 + \dots = \ln 2 \approx 0.693147.$$

The same argument with $\arctan x = \sum{k \geq 0} \frac{(-1)^k x^{2k+1}}{2k+1}$ gives $1 - \frac13 + \frac15 - \dots = \frac{\pi}{4}$. (These series converge slowly: the first 10 terms of the $\ln 2$ series sum to $0.6456$.)

> Common pitfall: Abel's theorem needs the series at $x = 1$ to converge. For $ak = (-1)^k$, $f(x) = \frac{1}{1 + x} \to \frac12$ as $x \to 1^-$, but $\sum (-1)^k$ diverges; the limit $\frac12$ is not a sum in the ordinary sense.

Chapter 5 — Taylor series, approximation, and what comes next

Taylor series versus the function

For a $C^\infty$ function $f$, the Taylor series at $0$ is $\sum \frac{f^{(k)}(0)}{k!}x^k$. Two things can go wrong:

1. The series can have radius $0$ (there are $C^\infty$ functions like this).2. The series can converge, to a different function.

Case 2 is the function of post 03: $f(x) = e^{-1/x^2}$ with $f(0) = 0$. All derivatives at $0$ vanish, so its Taylor series is $0 + 0x + 0x^2 + \dots$, with radius $\infty$ and sum $0$. It equals $f$ only at $x = 0$.

The link to Taylor's theorem is the remainder: the Taylor series of $f$ converges to $f(x)$ exactly when $Rn(x) \to 0$. For $e^x$ at any $x$, the Lagrange remainder satisfies $\lvert Rn(x)\rvert \leq e^{\lvert x\rvert}\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{n+1}}{(n+1)!} \to 0$, so $e^x$ equals its Taylor series everywhere. A function is real analytic at $x0$ when it equals a convergent power series on some interval around $x0$. By the term-by-term theorem, such a power series must be the Taylor series. So: analytic ⇒ $C^\infty$, but not conversely.

The Weierstrass approximation theorem

Not every continuous function is a power series, but every continuous function is uniformly close to a polynomial.

Theorem (Weierstrass, 1885). Let $f$ be continuous on $[a, b]$. For every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is a polynomial $p$ with $\lvert f(x) - p(x)\rvert < \varepsilon$ for all $x \in [a, b]$.

Bernstein's constructive proof (idea). On $[0, 1]$, define

$$Bn f(x) = \sum{k=0}^{n} f\!\left(\frac{k}{n}\right)\binom{n}{k} x^k (1 - x)^{n-k}.$$

The weights $\binom{n}{k}x^k(1-x)^{n-k}$ are the binomial probabilities of $k$ successes in $n$ trials with success probability $x$. They add up to $1$ and concentrate near $k/n \approx x$ as $n$ grows, so $Bn f(x)$ is a weighted average of values of $f$ near $x$. Uniform continuity of $f$ (post 02) turns this into $\lVert Bn f - f\rVert\infty \to 0$.

Worked example. For $f(x) = x^2$ a direct computation gives

$$Bn f(x) = x^2 + \frac{x(1 - x)}{n},$$

so the error is $\frac{x(1-x)}{n}$, largest at $x = \frac12$, and $\lVert Bn f - f\rVert\infty = \frac{1}{4n}$. An error of at most $0.01$ needs $n \geq 25$. The convergence is uniform but slow; Bernstein polynomials are valued for their shape-preserving properties (they are the basis of Bézier curves), not their speed.

Compare with the Weierstrass function of post 03: a continuous, nowhere-differentiable function can still be approximated uniformly, to any accuracy, by polynomials, which are as smooth as functions get. Uniform limits keep continuity and lose everything finer.

A look ahead

- Metric spaces. Uniform convergence is convergence in the metric $d(f, g) = \lVert f - g\rVert\infty$ on $C[a, b]$. Chapter 1's Cauchy criterion, together with the continuity theorem of Chapter 2, says this space is complete, and Weierstrass says polynomials are dense in it. The same words (complete, dense, compact) from posts 00–02 now describe spaces of functions.- Lebesgue integration. The spike example $n x(1 - x^2)^n$ shows that pointwise limits and integrals do not commute in general. Lebesgue's theory says exactly when they do (monotone and dominated convergence), without asking for uniformity.- Fourier series. Replacing powers $x^k$ by $\cos(kx)$ and $\sin(kx)$ gives Fourier series. Their convergence is subtler than for power series: the Fourier series of a discontinuous square wave converges pointwise at every point but never uniformly, which ties back to the continuity theorem of Chapter 2.

Practice Exercises

Exercise 1. Show that $fn(x) = \frac{x}{n}$ converges uniformly on $[0, 1]$ but not on $\mathbb{R}$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

The pointwise limit is $0$. On $[0, 1]$, $\lVert fn\rVert\infty = \frac1n \to 0$: uniform. On $\mathbb{R}$, $\supx \lvert x/n\rvert = \infty$ for every $n$ (take $x = n$ to get $fn(n) = 1$): not uniform.

</details>

Exercise 2. Let $fn(x) = x^n$. Prove that $fn \to 0$ uniformly on $[0, r]$ for each $r < 1$, and explain in one line why it cannot be uniform on $[0, 1)$ even though the limit $0$ is continuous there.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

On $[0, r]$, $\lVert x^n\rVert\infty = r^n \to 0$. On $[0, 1)$, the points $xn = (1/2)^{1/n}$ give $xn^n = \frac12$, so $\sup{[0,1)} x^n \geq \frac12$ for every $n$. (The continuity theorem only says a discontinuous limit rules out uniformity; a continuous limit does not guarantee it.)

</details>

Exercise 3. Use the M-test to show that $\sum{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{x^k}{k^2}$ converges uniformly on $[-1, 1]$. Is its sum continuous at $x = 1$?

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

For $\lvert x\rvert \leq 1$, $\left\lvert \frac{x^k}{k^2}\right\rvert \leq \frac1{k^2} = Mk$, and $\sum \frac1{k^2}$ converges. So the series converges uniformly on $[-1, 1]$ and the sum is continuous there, including at the endpoint $x = 1$ (one-sided).

</details>

Exercise 4. Find the radius of convergence of $\sum{k \geq 1} \frac{k^2}{3^k}x^k$ and of $\sum{k \geq 0} \frac{(2k)!}{(k!)^2}x^k$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

First: $\lvert ak\rvert^{1/k} = \frac{k^{2/k}}{3} \to \frac13$, so $R = 3$. Second, by the ratio formula: $\frac{a{k+1}}{ak} = \frac{(2k+2)(2k+1)}{(k+1)^2} \to 4$, so $R = \frac14$.

</details>

Exercise 5. Compute $\sum{k \geq 1} \frac{k}{3^k}$ using term-by-term differentiation.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

From $\sum{k \geq 1} k x^{k} = \frac{x}{(1 - x)^2}$ for $\lvert x\rvert < 1$, put $x = \frac13$: $\frac{1/3}{(2/3)^2} = \frac{1/3}{4/9} = \frac34$.

</details>

Exercise 6. For $fn(x) = n x (1 - x^2)^n$ on $[0, 1]$, compute $\int0^1 fn$ for $n = 10$ and explain why this does not contradict the integral theorem.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

$\int0^1 fn = \frac{n}{2(n+1)}$, so for $n = 10$ it is $\frac{10}{22} = \frac{5}{11} \approx 0.4545$. The pointwise limit is $0$ with integral $0$. No contradiction: the convergence is not uniform (the maximum of $f{10}$ is about $1.34$ and the maxima grow without bound), so the theorem does not apply.

</details>

Exercise 7. Show that the Bernstein polynomial $Bn f$ of $f(x) = x$ is $x$ itself for every $n$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

$Bn f(x) = \sum{k=0}^{n} \frac{k}{n}\binom{n}{k}x^k(1 - x)^{n-k}$. This is $\frac1n$ times the mean of a binomial distribution with $n$ trials and success probability $x$, which is $nx$. So $Bn f(x) = x$. (Bernstein polynomials reproduce linear functions exactly; the first error appears for $x^2$.)

</details>

Cheat-sheet

| Idea | Statement ||---|---|| Pointwise | for each $x$, $fn(x) \to f(x)$; $N$ may depend on $x$ || Uniform | $\lVert fn - f\rVert\infty \to 0$; one $N$ for all $x$ || Key example | $x^n$ on $[0,1]$: pointwise, not uniform; uniform on $[0, r]$, $r < 1$ || Uniform Cauchy | $\lvert fn(x) - fm(x)\rvert < \varepsilon$ for all $x$, $m, n \geq N$ || Continuity | uniform limit of continuous is continuous ($\varepsilon/3$) || Integrals | uniform on $[a,b]$ $\Rightarrow \int fn \to \int f$ || Derivatives | need $fn'$ uniform plus one convergent $fn(x0)$ || M-test | $\lvert uk\rvert \leq Mk$, $\sum Mk < \infty \Rightarrow$ uniform || Radius | $1/R = \limsup \lvert ak\rvert^{1/k}$; or $R = \lim \lvert ak/a{k+1}\rvert$ || Inside the radius | uniform on $[-r, r]$ for $r < R$; not always on $(-R, R)$ || Term by term | $f'$ and $\int f$ by term, same $R$; $ak = f^{(k)}(0)/k!$ || Abel | $\sum ak$ converges $\Rightarrow \lim{x \to 1^-}\sum ak x^k = \sum ak$ || Smooth, not analytic | $e^{-1/x^2}$: Taylor series is $0$ || Weierstrass approximation | continuous on $[a,b]$ ⇒ uniform limit of polynomials || Bernstein for $x^2$ | error $x(1-x)/n$, sup $1/(4n)$ |

Learning Outcome

- Explain what "concurrent" means in React — interruptible rendering on one thread — and what it does not mean.- Describe lanes at a high level and how React maps events to priorities.- Use  /  to keep input responsive, and know their limits (async , text inputs).- Use  to defer an expensive subtree, and compare it with debouncing.- Explain how Suspense works for code and data, and why transitions stop already-shown content from being replaced by a fallback.- Build forms with React 19 Actions: , , .- Use  for promises and context, and know the cached-promise rule.- Use  to keep hidden UI alive without running its effects.- Draw the server/client boundary of React Server Components and name what can cross it.- Decide how to adopt React Compiler 1.0 in an existing codebase.

Chapter 1 — Concurrent rendering

Terminology

- Blocking (urgent) update — must be shown right away: typing, clicking, pressing a key. Rendered synchronously to the end.- Non-blocking update (Transition) — may take a moment: switching tabs, filtering a big list, navigating. Rendered in time slices and can be interrupted.- Interruptible render — React can stop building the  tree (Post 00), handle something more urgent, then restart or resume.- Tearing — part of the UI showing one version of the data and part another. Concurrent rendering must avoid it (Post 01's ).

What "concurrent" means

JavaScript in the browser still runs on one main thread. React does not render in parallel. "Concurrent" means React can keep several versions of the UI in progress and choose which to work on:

1. The user types in a search box while a large result list is being re-rendered as a Transition.2. The keystroke arrives. React pauses (or abandons) the list render at the next yield point.3. It renders and commits the input change synchronously, so the character appears at once.4. It restarts the list render with the newest query.

Without concurrent rendering, step 2 is impossible: a render that started must finish, and a 200 ms list render means 200 ms of input lag.

What concurrent rendering does not do: make a slow component faster. The total work is the same (or a little more, because of restarts). It only changes which work the user waits for.

Opting in. There is no global switch.  enables the concurrent renderer, but updates stay blocking unless you mark them: , , or Suspense-related features. An app that uses none of these behaves much like React 17, plus automatic batching.

Chapter 2 — Lanes at a high level

React tracks priority with lanes: bits in a 31-bit integer. Each update gets a lane; each fiber records the lanes of its pending updates (,  from Post 00). A bit mask makes it cheap to ask "does this subtree have work at this priority?" and to process several updates of the same priority together.

The main lanes, from most to least urgent (names from the React source; internals, may change):

| Lane (group) | Typical source ||---|---||  | Discrete events: , , , ;  ||  | Continuous events: , , ,  ||  | Updates from outside React events: , promise callbacks, initial render ||  (several) | , , 's background render ||  | Retrying a Suspense boundary after its data arrives ||  | Idle work ||  | Hidden content, e.g. a hidden  |

React-DOM picks the lane for an update from the event that is being handled when  is called. That is why the same  call is urgent in a click handler and lower priority in a .

Three consequences worth knowing:

- Higher lanes interrupt lower ones. A Transition render in progress is paused or thrown away when a Sync update arrives.- Transitions are batched. As of React 19.2, when several Transitions are pending, React can render them together, so a slow Transition can delay a fast one.- Starvation is prevented. A lower-priority lane that keeps being interrupted is eventually marked expired and rendered synchronously, so it cannot be delayed forever.

> Trick: the Performance panel in Chrome shows React's "Scheduler" and "Components" tracks (React 19.2 Performance Tracks), labelled with priorities such as blocking and transition. It is the quickest way to see lanes in a real trace.

Async Transitions (React 19)

In React 19 the function passed to  can be async. The whole async function is an Action;  stays  until it finishes:

The inner  is there because of a known limitation documented on react.dev: state updates after an  are not automatically part of the Transition. React loses the async context at the , so wrap later updates in another .

Limits

- Do not use a Transition for a controlled text input's own value. The input must update synchronously; put only the expensive consequence (the filtered list) behind the Transition, or use .- Only  calls made synchronously inside the callback (or inside nested ) are marked. A  inside  escapes it.- Out-of-order responses are your problem with raw async Transitions: two quick submits can finish in the wrong order.  (Chapter 6) queues actions and avoids this.

> Watch out: a Transition does not make a slow component faster. If  takes 800 ms to render, the user still waits 800 ms for it; they just are not frozen while waiting. Pair Transitions with the performance work in Post 03.

Chapter 4 —

is the Transition tool for when you do not own the  call — typically a value that arrives as a prop.

On each keystroke React first renders with the old deferred value (fast, because  is memoised and its prop did not change), commits the input, then renders again in the background with the new value. If another key arrives, the background render is abandoned.

Two details matter:

-  (or the React Compiler) is required. Without it,  re-renders in the urgent pass too, and nothing is gained.- React 19 added an  argument: . On the first render React uses , then renders again in the background with the real value — useful for showing a cheap placeholder first.

Deferred value vs debounce

| |  | Debounce (e.g. 300 ms) ||---|---|---|| Delay | None on a fast device; adapts to how slow rendering is | Fixed, even when rendering is fast || Interruptible | Yes: stale background renders are abandoned | No: once the render starts it blocks || Network requests | Does not reduce them by itself | Reduces them |

They solve different problems.  protects rendering; debouncing protects the network. A search box that calls an API often uses both: debounce the request, defer the rendering.

Chapter 5 — Suspense for code and data

What Suspense does

A  boundary shows its fallback while something inside it is not ready. Children signal "not ready" by suspending. Supported sources:

-  components while their code loads (Post 03),-  while the promise is pending,- Suspense-enabled data libraries and frameworks (TanStack Query's , Relay, Next.js and other RSC frameworks).

Suspense does not detect a fetch inside . The data source has to integrate with it.

Nested boundaries define the loading sequence: the header can appear as soon as its data arrives, while the posts area keeps its own skeleton. Place boundaries where a designer would put a loading state, not around every component.

Suspense and Transitions

When a component that is already showing suspends again (for example because a new  needs new data), React has two choices: replace the content with the fallback, or keep the old content until the new content is ready.

- In a blocking update, React shows the nearest fallback. Existing content disappears — usually a bad experience.- In a Transition, React keeps the old UI on screen and waits; only new boundaries that have never shown content will show their fallback.

That is why routers built for React 18+ wrap navigations in : clicking a link keeps the current page visible (with  for a progress bar) instead of flashing a skeleton.

To force a fresh fallback for a different entity, change the boundary's identity: .

React 19 changes

- Sibling pre-warming. When a component suspends, React 19 commits the nearest fallback immediately, without first rendering the rest of the siblings, and then renders the suspended siblings in the background to start their lazy requests.- SSR reveal batching (19.2). Server-rendered Suspense boundaries that resolve close together are revealed in a short batch instead of one by one, which matches client behaviour and prepares for view-transition animations.

> Common pitfall: creating the promise during render:  in a client component creates a new promise every render, so it never resolves from React's point of view. Promises passed to  must be cached — created by a framework, a data library, or a Server Component and passed down.

Chapter 6 — Actions: , ,

Terminology

- Action — a function, usually async, run inside a Transition. React tracks its pending state, errors and optimistic updates.- Form action — a function passed to  (or ). React calls it with the  inside a Transition and, when it succeeds, resets uncontrolled fields.- Server Function — an async function marked  that runs on the server but can be called from the client (called "Server Action" when used as an action).

What you no longer write: the  flag, the  to reset it, the error state, the . Calls are queued: each call receives the state returned by the previous one, so fast repeated submits cannot finish out of order. With a Server Function as  and a framework that supports it, the form also works before JavaScript loads;  tells React which URL the form goes to in that case.

A design-system button that knows whether its parent form is submitting, without prop drilling:

It reads the status of the parent  only, so it must be used in a component rendered inside the form — not in the component that renders the form.

The optimistic state exists only while the Action is pending. When the Action ends, React renders with whatever  is then — the real list if the save succeeded and the data was refreshed, or the old list if it threw. There is no manual rollback code. The setter must be called inside an Action (a form action or ); outside one, React warns and the optimistic value only flashes.

> Watch out: if the Action succeeds but nothing updates  (no refetch, no parent state change), the optimistic item disappears when the Action ends. Optimistic UI needs a real data update behind it.

Chapter 8 —  (React 19.2)

hides part of the UI without unmounting it:

When , React:

- hides the children with  — the DOM stays, so scroll position, input text and  elements are kept;- keeps their state;- cleans up their effects (subscriptions stop, timers clear);- keeps rendering them when their props change, but at a lower priority than visible content.

When the boundary becomes visible again, effects are set up again and the UI appears with its state intact. Compared with , switching back is instant and nothing is lost. A hidden  can also pre-render content the user is likely to open next, at low priority.

Things to check before using it:

- Effects must be written correctly (Post 01): hiding runs their cleanup, showing runs setup again. This is exactly what StrictMode's double effect run rehearses.- DOM side effects do not stop automatically. A playing  keeps playing when hidden unless an effect cleanup pauses it.- Hidden trees still cost memory. Do not keep dozens of large screens alive.

Chapter 9 — React Server Components: an overview

React Server Components (RSC) split the component tree between two environments. This chapter gives the React-level model; the Next.js Deep Dive series covers routing, caching and deployment.

The rules

- Server Components are the default in an RSC framework. They run on the server (at build time or per request), can be , can read files and databases directly, and send no JavaScript for themselves to the browser. They cannot use state, effects, or browser APIs.-  at the top of a file marks a boundary: that module and everything it imports become part of the client bundle. Client Components render on the server for HTML (SSR) and then hydrate in the browser.- Props from a Server Component to a Client Component must be serialisable: primitives, plain objects and arrays, , , , typed arrays, promises, JSX elements, and Server Functions. Ordinary functions and class instances cannot cross.-  marks Server Functions: async functions the client can call over the network (for example as a form action). Treat each one as a public API endpoint — validate input and check authorisation inside it.

A Client Component can receive Server Components as  (the composition pattern from Post 00): the server renders them and the client only places the result. That is how interactive shells wrap server-rendered content without pulling it into the client bundle.

> Common pitfall: putting  at the top of a high-level layout "to make things work". Everything imported below it becomes client code, and the bundle-size benefit of RSC is gone. Push the boundary down to the leaves that need interactivity.

Chapter 10 — React Compiler 1.0

React Compiler reached 1.0 on 7 October 2025. It is a build-time tool (a Babel plugin, , with experimental SWC support) that adds automatic memoisation to components and hooks.

What it does

The compiler analyses each component and hook, works out which values depend on which inputs, and caches JSX, objects, callbacks and computed values so they keep their identity until their inputs change. It can memoise in places where  cannot be written, such as after an early return, and at a finer grain than most hand-written code.

The practical effect: the "new object prop breaks " and "inline callback re-renders the child" problems from Posts 00 and 01 mostly disappear, without writing ,  or .

What it needs

It assumes the Rules of React: pure render, no mutation of props or state, hooks at the top level, no reading refs during render. When it cannot prove a component follows the rules, it skips that component and leaves it as written. The compiler-powered rules in  (v6+, in the  preset) — , , , ,  and others — report the code it would skip.

Adopting it

| Setting | Meaning ||---|---||  (default) | Compile functions that look like components (PascalCase, return JSX) or hooks ( prefix) ||  | Compile only functions with a  directive — for gradual rollout ||  directive | Always skip this function — an escape hatch while fixing a problem ||  /  | Supports React 17 and 18 with the  package |

Framework support: Next.js 16 made the  option stable (not on by default; builds get slower because it uses Babel), Expo SDK 54 enables it by default, and  offers a compiler template.

A realistic rollout:

1. Turn on the lint rules first and fix what they report.2. Enable the compiler for one directory or with  mode.3. Run the tests and profile key screens (Post 03).4. Widen the scope.

The React team's guidance on existing  / : for new code, rely on the compiler and use them only where precise control is needed (for example, a value used as an effect dependency); for existing code, leave them in place or remove them carefully with tests, because removing them can change the compiled output.

> Watch out: the compiler makes it easy to forget that memoisation is about identity. Code that depends on a value keeping its identity for correctness (an effect that must not re-run) should still say so explicitly, not rely on the compiler's current heuristics.

Exercise 3 — Why did the optimistic item vanish?

A "like" button uses  to show . After the request succeeds, the count goes back to the old number. Why?

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

The optimistic value only lives while the Action is pending. When it ends, React renders from the real  — which never changed, because nothing refetched or updated the like count after the save. Update the source state (set it from the response, invalidate the query, or refresh the server data) inside the Action.

</details>

Exercise 4 — Find the boundary

A Server Component page passes  to a Client Component . The build fails. Explain and fix.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

A plain function is not serialisable, so it cannot cross from server to client. Move the handler into the Client Component (it can call  itself), or, if the work belongs on the server, pass a Server Function marked .

</details>

Exercise 5 — Tabs that keep their state

A settings screen has three tabs; switching tabs loses scroll position and half-filled inputs. Fix it with React 19.2, and name one thing to check in the tab components first.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Render all three inside . State and DOM are kept; effects are cleaned up while hidden. Check that each tab's effects have correct cleanups (and that nothing like a media element keeps running), because hiding runs cleanup and showing runs setup again.

</details>

In those moments your computer found an IP address, opened a TCP connection, agreed on encryption keys with a server it has never met, and checked that server's identity against a chain of certificates. Only then did it send the first HTTP request. This post walks through each step and shows the commands that let you watch it happen, in Bash and PowerShell. It ends with a field guide to the HTTPS errors you will actually meet.

Chapter 1 — The big picture

The whole trip for :

1. Parse the URL. Scheme , host , default port , path .2. HSTS check. If the browser has a Strict-Transport-Security rule for this host, it never uses plain  (Chapter 7).3. DNS. Turn  into an IP address (Chapter 2).4. TCP. Open a reliable connection to that IP on port 443 (Chapter 3).5. TLS. Agree on keys, check the certificate, switch to encryption (Chapters 4-5).6. HTTP. Send  inside the encrypted channel and read the response.

A round trip time (RTT) is the time for a packet to reach the server and come back. Every handshake costs round trips. Round trips are what make a far-away server feel slow.

can print a timer for each phase, measured from the start of the request:

DNS took about 6 ms (it was cached). TCP was done at 192 ms, TLS at 1.89 s, and the first response byte (TTFB, time to first byte) came at 2.10 s. To get the length of one phase, subtract the value before it.

Chapter 2 — DNS: from name to IP address

Packets are routed by IP address, not by name. DNS (RFC 1034/1035) is the distributed phone book that turns names into addresses.

There are 13 named root server identities ( to ), each served from many sites by anycast. The authoritative server holds the real records, and is usually run by your DNS host or CDN.

Every answer carries a TTL (time to live) in seconds. Caches may reuse the answer until the TTL runs out. That is why a DNS change takes time to reach everyone: old answers live until their TTL ends.

> Trick: before you move a site to a new IP, lower the TTL (for example to 300 s) a day ahead. After the move, raise it again.

Run it again a minute later and the TTL is lower: the answer came from a cache that is counting down.  in PowerShell 5.1 and the Windows  do not know the  type, so use  (Linux, macOS, WSL) for that one.

| Type | Holds | Why it matters for HTTPS ||------|-------|--------------------------||  /  | IPv4 / IPv6 address | Where to connect. ||  | Alias to another name | Common for CDNs ( → ). ||  | CAs allowed to issue for the domain (RFC 8659) | A CA must check it before issuing. ||  | Free text | Used by the ACME  challenge (Chapter 8). ||  /  | Connection hints: ALPN list, ECH keys (RFC 9460) | Tells the browser about HTTP/3 and ECH before it connects. |

> Watch out: classic DNS is plain text, so anyone on the path can see which names you look up. DNS over HTTPS (DoH, RFC 8484) and DNS over TLS (DoT, RFC 7858) encrypt the query between you and the recursive resolver.

Chapter 3 — TCP: the three-way handshake

HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2 run over TCP, which gives an ordered, reliable byte stream. Opening it takes three packets:

That is one full round trip before any data flows. To test only the TCP step:

A timeout usually means a firewall, a wrong IP (stale DNS), or a server that is down. A fast connection refused means the host is reachable but nothing listens on that port. If TCP fails, TLS never starts.

Chapter 4 — The TLS 1.3 handshake

TLS (Transport Layer Security) gives three things: confidentiality (only the two ends can read), integrity (changes on the way are detected), and authentication (you reached the real ).

TLS 1.3 was defined in RFC 8446 (August 2018). In July 2026 the IETF published RFC 9846, which obsoletes RFC 8446. It calls itself a minor update that keeps the version number 1.3 and stays backward compatible. It also obsoletes RFC 5246, the TLS 1.2 spec.

1. ClientHello. The client guesses which key-exchange group the server accepts and sends a public key for it at once. This guess is what saves a round trip compared with TLS 1.2.2. ServerHello. The server sends its own public key. Both sides now run ECDHE (Elliptic-Curve Diffie-Hellman, Ephemeral): each mixes its private key with the other's public key and gets the same shared secret. Someone watching the wire cannot compute it.3. Keys are derived from that secret. Everything after ServerHello is encrypted, including the certificate.4. CertificateVerify. The server signs a hash of all handshake messages so far with the private key of its certificate. This proves it owns the certificate, not just a copy.5. Finished. Each side sends a MAC over the whole transcript. If an attacker changed any earlier message, the MACs do not match and the connection dies.6. The client sends its HTTP request together with its Finished. Total: one round trip (1-RTT).

The key pairs are ephemeral: new for each connection, then thrown away. So stealing the server's certificate key later does not decrypt recorded old sessions. This is forward secrecy.

means AES-256 in GCM mode (an AEAD cipher: it encrypts and checks integrity in one step) with SHA-384 for key derivation.  is the ephemeral ECDHE group. That output came from OpenSSL 3.2. From OpenSSL 3.5 on, the default key shares offer the hybrid post-quantum group  (classic X25519 plus ML-KEM-768) as well as X25519, so you may see that name instead.

Resumption and 0-RTT

After a handshake the server can send a session ticket. Next time the client presents it as a pre-shared key (PSK) and may send data in its very first flight: 0-RTT early data.

> Watch out: 0-RTT data can be replayed. An attacker who records the first flight can send it again, and the server may act on it twice. RFC 8446 says 0-RTT data has no non-replay guarantee between connections, and it is not forward secret either. Allow it only for safe, idempotent requests such as . RFC 8470 defines the  header and the  status for HTTP servers that want to refuse it.

Terminology

| Term | Meaning ||------|---------|| Key share | Public key sent in a hello message for key exchange. || ECDHE | Key agreement: both sides derive the same secret from their key pairs. || Forward secrecy | Old sessions stay safe even if the long-term key leaks later. || AEAD | Cipher giving confidentiality and integrity together (AES-GCM, ChaCha20-Poly1305). || Transcript | All handshake messages so far; signed and MAC'd to stop tampering. || PSK / 0-RTT | Resumption key / data sent before the handshake finishes. |

Chapter 5 — Certificates and the chain of trust

Key exchange gives encryption, but encryption with whom? The certificate answers that. It is a statement, "this public key belongs to ", signed by a Certificate Authority (CA).

- Subject Alternative Name (SAN) — the host names the certificate is valid for. Clients check the SAN, not the old Common Name.- notBefore / notAfter — the validity window. Outside it, the certificate is rejected.- Issuer — who signed it. The issuer has its own certificate, and so on up the chain.

The server sends the leaf plus the intermediates. It does not send the root: the client must already have it in its trust store. The client builds a path from the leaf to a trusted root and checks every signature, the dates, the host name against the SAN, and key-usage limits. On Windows you can build the same chain with .NET (PowerShell 5.1):

It prints  and the same four names, leaf first. Trust stores differ by client: Windows and Chrome/Edge on Windows use the Windows certificate store (); macOS uses the keychain; Firefox has its own Mozilla store; Linux tools use a distro bundle; Python  uses the  bundle. So one site can pass in one tool and fail in another.

How long a certificate may live — the rules today

Public TLS certificates follow the CA/Browser Forum Baseline Requirements. Ballot SC-081v3, adopted on April 11, 2025, shrinks the maximum validity in steps (Baseline Requirements, section 6.3.2):

| Certificate issued on or after | Issued before | Maximum validity ||--------------------------------|---------------|------------------|| — | 2026-03-15 | 398 days || 2026-03-15 | 2027-03-15 | 200 days ← applies now || 2027-03-15 | 2029-03-15 | 100 days || 2029-03-15 | — | 47 days |

The same ballot cuts how long a CA may reuse a domain-validation result: 200 days from 2026-03-15, 100 days from 2027-03-15, 10 days from 2029-03-15. So a certificate issued today lives at most 200 days, and yearly manual renewal is already gone.

Revocation. If a key leaks, the CA can revoke the certificate and publish that in a CRL (signed list of revoked serial numbers) or via OCSP (ask about one certificate). Let's Encrypt turned off its OCSP service on August 6, 2025, and now publishes revocation data only through CRLs. Short lifetimes also limit damage: a leaked short-lived certificate soon stops working anyway.

> Trick: every publicly trusted certificate is recorded in public Certificate Transparency logs. Search your domain on a CT search site such as crt.sh to see every certificate ever issued for it.

Chapter 6 — SNI, ECH, ALPN, HTTP/2 and HTTP/3

SNI. One IP often serves thousands of sites (think of a CDN). The server must pick a certificate before any HTTP  header exists. So the client puts the host name in the ClientHello: the Server Name Indication extension (RFC 6066). That is why  needs . Without it you may get a default certificate for another name.

ECH. SNI is plain text, so the network can see which site you visit. Encrypted Client Hello encrypts the real ClientHello under a public key the site publishes in its  DNS record.

- RFC 9849, TLS Encrypted Client Hello, was published in March 2026 (Standards Track). RFC 9848, the same month, defines how ECH settings travel in SVCB/HTTPS DNS records.- Chrome Platform Status lists ECH as enabled by default since Chrome 117. Firefox 119 release notes say ECH "is now available to Firefox users".- OpenSSL 4.0, released April 14, 2026, added ECH support.- The site's server or CDN must support it too. Where it does not, the connection falls back to normal SNI.

ALPN. Application-Layer Protocol Negotiation (RFC 7301) lets the client list protocols such as  and  in the ClientHello. The server picks one in its reply, with no extra round trip.

| | HTTP/1.1 | HTTP/2 (RFC 9113) | HTTP/3 (RFC 9114) ||---|---|---|---|| Transport | TCP + TLS | TCP + TLS | QUIC over UDP (RFC 9000) || Requests per connection | One at a time | Many streams | Many streams || One lost packet stalls | That connection | All streams (TCP head-of-line blocking) | Only its own stream || Setup | TCP 1 RTT + TLS 1 RTT | Same | Transport + TLS 1.3 together, 1 RTT |

QUIC carries TLS 1.3 inside its own handshake (RFC 9001). A browser learns that a site speaks HTTP/3 from the  DNS record or from an  response header. The one from google.com begins with  ("HTTP/3 on port 443, remember this for 30 days").

> Watch out:  works only if your curl build has HTTP/3. Check the Features line of  for . Neither the Git Bash curl nor the Windows  used for this post had it.

Chapter 7 — HSTS: never talk plain HTTP again

A user types  with no scheme. The browser tries , and the server redirects to . That first plain request can be intercepted. An attacker on public Wi-Fi can keep the user on HTTP ("SSL stripping").

HTTP Strict Transport Security (RFC 6797) closes the gap. The server sends, over HTTPS:

(That exact header comes from github.com.) For  seconds (here one year) the browser turns every  URL for the host into  before sending anything. It also stops users from clicking through certificate errors.  applies the rule to every subdomain.  agrees to be built into browsers' preload list, which also protects the very first visit. To join that list, hstspreload.org requires a valid certificate, an HTTP → HTTPS redirect, HTTPS on all subdomains, and a header with  of at least 31536000,  and .

> Common pitfall: adding  while some old subdomain (an intranet box, a printer page) only speaks HTTP. Browsers will refuse it. The preload site warns that removal takes months to reach users. Start with a short , check every subdomain, then raise it.

Chapter 8 — Let's Encrypt and ACME

Let's Encrypt is a free, automated CA. Clients talk to it with ACME (Automatic Certificate Management Environment, RFC 8555):

1. The client creates an account key and places an order for names, e.g.  and .2. For each name the CA sets a challenge to prove control:-  — serve a token at .-  — publish a  record at . Needed for wildcard certificates.-  — answer a special TLS handshake on port 443 (RFC 8737).3. The client finalizes with a CSR (certificate signing request) holding its public key.4. The CA issues the certificate; the client installs it and reloads the server.

On Windows servers, win-acme does the same job. Caddy, and hosts such as GitHub Pages, Cloudflare and AWS Certificate Manager, run ACME for you.

Let's Encrypt profiles today (from its profiles page):

| Profile | Validity | Notes ||---------|----------|-------||  | 90 days | The default. ||  | 45 days | For subscribers who "fully embrace automation". ||  | 160 hours (just over 6 days) | Generally available since January 15, 2026; opt-in; required for IP-address certificates. |

The announced plan for the default: on February 10, 2027,  moves to 64-day certificates with a 10-day authorization reuse period. On February 16, 2028 it moves to 45 days with a 7-hour reuse period. That stays under the 47-day cap that starts March 15, 2029.

ACME Renewal Information (ARI), RFC 9773 (June 2025), lets the CA tell your client when to renew, for example early during an incident. Let's Encrypt recommends ARI and renewing about two-thirds of the way through a certificate's life.

> Trick: alert when any certificate has less than a third of its life left. Automation fails quietly: a changed firewall rule, an expired DNS API token. Exercise 3 builds a checker.

Chapter 9 — Common HTTPS errors and how to diagnose them

| Symptom (Chrome) |  verify result | Usual cause | Fix ||------------------|-------------------------|-------------|-----||  |  | Renewal did not run, or the server was not reloaded | Renew, reload, find out why automation failed ||  |  | Name not in SAN; wrong virtual host; no SNI | Add the name; check  / SNI || Fails in some clients only |  | Server sends the leaf without intermediates | Serve the full chain () ||  |  /  | Self-signed cert, private CA, TLS-inspecting proxy | Public CA, or install the private root on purpose ||  on a good site |  | Your clock is wrong | Fix the system time |

The site badssl.com hosts broken endpoints on purpose, so you can practise safely:

The same check in Python, with days left:

Why the missing intermediate is so confusing.  failed on , but Python on Windows said OK, and so did the Windows  (Schannel). Windows can download a missing intermediate itself, from the "CA Issuers" (AIA) URL inside the leaf, and it caches the result. Python on Windows loads certificates from the Windows stores, so it most likely found that cached copy. Many clients never do this: , many Linux tools, Java, some mobile apps. So the site "works on my machine" and fails for others.

> Common pitfall: testing only in your desktop browser. Always test the chain with , which does not fetch missing intermediates, or with an online SSL checker.

Clock skew. A certificate is valid only between  and . A clock that is a year behind makes every new certificate "not yet valid". A clock that is ahead makes them look expired. Suspects: dead CMOS battery, fresh VMs, devices without network time. Check with  on Windows ( in an admin shell forces a sync) or  on Linux ("System clock synchronized: yes"). More in Dev Essentials 02 · Dates & Time Zones.

In the browser. In Chrome or Edge, click the icon left of the address and open the connection details to view the certificate. DevTools has a Security panel (called "Privacy and security" in some recent versions) with the protocol, key exchange, cipher and a View certificate button. In Firefox: padlock → Connection secure → More information → View Certificate.

> Watch out: never "fix" an error by turning verification off: , , . That removes the authentication part of TLS, and any man-in-the-middle can then read and change your traffic. Fix the certificate, or add your private CA to the trust store on purpose.

Practice Exercises

Exercise 1 — Time each phase

Run  against three sites: one near you, one far away, one behind a CDN. Work out DNS, TCP and TLS time separately. Which phases grow with distance?

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

TCP time is , and TLS time is . Each costs about one RTT, so both grow with distance. DNS is often near zero after the first run because of caching. A CDN cuts TCP and TLS time because the handshake ends at an edge server near you.

</details>

Exercise 2 — Read a chain

Run  against github.com. Which certificates did the server send, and which one did it not send?

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Line  is the leaf and line  the intermediate. Line  is the newer Sectigo E46 root cross-signed by the older USERTrust root, which helps older clients that only trust USERTrust. The top root (USERTrust) is not sent: the client must already trust it, and an untrusted root would not help.

</details>

Exercise 3 — Expiry monitor

Change the Chapter 9 checker so it reads host names from a file and exits with code 1 if any certificate fails or has fewer than 30 days left. A scheduled task or CI job can then alert on it.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Run , then check  (Bash) or  (PowerShell). A fixed 30 days is too long for 6-day certificates. A threshold of one-third of the lifetime scales better.

</details>

Exercise 4 — Design an HSTS header

is fully HTTPS, except , an HTTP-only admin box that will be retired in three months. What HSTS header should  send today?

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Leave out  and  until  is gone or on HTTPS. Otherwise browsers that visited  would refuse plain HTTP to . After the retirement, add , test, then think about .

</details>

Exercise 5 — 0-RTT risk

A banking API accepts 0-RTT early data. Why is  in early data dangerous, and what should the server do?

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Early data can be replayed: an attacker who captured the first flight can send it again, and the transfer may run twice. The server should accept only idempotent requests (such as ) as early data. For anything else it should refuse early data, for example with  (RFC 8470), so the client retries after the full handshake. The request should also carry an idempotency key (see Dev Essentials 14 · API Reliability).

</details>
