> Note: this post is in progress. Planned additions: a Wireshark walkthrough of a TLS 1.3 handshake (with ), mutual TLS with client certificates, and a closer look at post-quantum key exchange.
14:T8c25,Almost every dramatic 3D printing failure is a boring first-layer failure that was allowed to continue for six hours. Post 00 established what the machine does physically; this post makes it do it repeatably. The organising idea is that calibration is not a ritual you perform once and forget, but a short ordered sequence of measurements — each one produces a number you write down, and each one must be done before the next because the next depends on it. Copying settings from a forum post skips the measurement and therefore skips the calibration entirely.

> Series map: 00 How 3D Printing Works → 01 First Print and Calibration (this post) → 02 Slicing in Depth → 03 Orientation, Supports and Design for Printing → 04 Materials → 05 Dimensional Accuracy and Tolerances → 06 CAD for Printing → 07 Strength, Failure Modes and Functional Parts.

Learning Outcome

- State the first-layer doctrine and explain mechanically why the first layer determines most print outcomes.- Tram a bed, set a Z-offset by the paper method and by feeler gauge, and explain what mesh bed levelling does and does not fix.- Compare probe technologies (inductive, BLTouch-style, eddy current, strain gauge) by what each actually senses.- Choose a build surface and a cleaning regime, and explain why a fingerprint is an adhesion failure.- Read a first-layer test print and say precisely whether the nozzle is too high, too low, or correct.- Execute the calibration sequence in the correct order, taking a real measurement at each step: rotation distance/e-steps, temperature, flow, retraction, pressure advance, maximum volumetric flow, cooling.- Explain which settings belong to the printer and which belong to the spool.- Diagnose the common first-week failures from their visual signature.

Chapter 1 — The first layer: doctrine, tramming and Z-offset

Terminology

- Tramming — physically adjusting the bed so its plane is parallel to the plane the nozzle travels in. Often loosely called "levelling", which is misleading: gravity is irrelevant, parallelism is everything.- Z-offset — the distance between the point the printer calls Z = 0 and the point where the nozzle actually touches the bed. A single scalar, and the most consequential number on the machine.- Nozzle-to-bed gap — the physical clearance while printing the first layer. Deliberately smaller than the first-layer height, so that material is squashed.- Squish — the intentional compression of the first layer. It is what converts a round extrudate into a flat road with a wide contact patch.- Mesh bed levelling — probing a grid of points and compensating for bed shape by modulating Z during the print.- Probe repeatability — the spread of readings when the probe measures the same point many times. The floor on how good your mesh can be.- Elephant foot — the outward bulge at the bottom of a part caused by excessive first-layer squish, excessive bed temperature, or both.

Why the first layer decides everything

Three separate mechanisms, all rooted in the same event:

1. Adhesion is contact area. A squashed road has a wide flat contact patch against the plate; an unsquashed one touches along a line. Adhesion force scales with that contact patch, so a nozzle 50 µm too high can halve it.2. The first layer is the datum for every layer above it. Layer 2 is deposited onto layer 1 at a fixed Z step. If layer 1 is thinner than commanded, layer 2 is deposited into empty space and the error propagates upward as under-extrusion that never recovers. If layer 1 is thicker than commanded, layer 2 collides with it.3. Warping starts at the corners of the first layer. As upper layers cool and contract, they pull inward on the part. That force is resisted only by the bond between first layer and plate. A weak first layer does not resist it, so the corner lifts, and once a corner lifts the nozzle starts hitting the raised edge.

The operational rule: watch the first layer finish before you leave the room. Nothing else in this hobby has a better ratio of attention spent to failures avoided.

Step 1: mechanical prerequisites, before any of this

Levelling compensates for shape. It cannot compensate for movement. Before touching Z-offset:

- Check that the bed does not rock. Press each corner; if it moves, springs are too loose or the mounts are worn.- Check belt tension (X and Y): a belt should feel taut, and plucking it should give a defined note rather than a dull thud. Loose belts show up later as ringing and dimensional error that no calibration will fix.- Check that the gantry is square and, on dual-Z machines, that both Z motors are synchronised — Marlin's  and Klipper's  /  exist for exactly this.- Check the nozzle is clean and cold-pulled free of debris. A blob of burnt plastic on the nozzle tip makes every subsequent measurement meaningless.- Heat everything to printing temperature before probing. Beds change shape by a measurable amount between cold and 60 °C; probes drift with temperature. Probing cold and printing hot is a common self-inflicted wound.

Step 2: tram the bed

Even with automatic levelling, the bed should be physically parallel to within a few tenths of a millimetre. On a four-screw bed the classic procedure is manual and works:

1. Heat bed and nozzle to the temperatures you will print at.2. Home all axes, then disable the steppers' Z compensation ( on Marlin, or simply skip mesh loading).3. Move the nozzle over screw 1, drop to Z = 0, adjust the screw until a sheet of paper drags with light, consistent friction.4. Repeat for screws 2, 3, 4, then repeat the whole cycle at least twice — adjusting one corner moves the others.5. Check the centre. If the centre is markedly high or low relative to all four corners, the bed is dished or crowned; that is what mesh compensation is for.

Some machines use screw-tilt-calibrate helpers ( in Klipper,  in Marlin) that probe and tell you how far and which way to turn each screw. Use them if available — they replace a subjective feel with a number.

Step 3: set the Z-offset

Two methods, both legitimate:

The paper method. A sheet of ordinary office paper is roughly 0.1 mm thick, which is conveniently close to a good first-layer gap. Lower Z in 0.02–0.05 mm steps until the paper can still be moved but drags noticeably. The weakness is that "drags noticeably" is subjective, that paper thickness varies by brand, and that the paper compresses under the nozzle.

A feeler gauge. A 0.1 mm feeler gauge is a known, incompressible thickness. Same procedure, but the result is a number you can reproduce next month. If you intend to be systematic, buy a feeler gauge set.

Live adjustment during the first layer. The most practical method on a machine that supports baby-stepping: start a large first-layer test, and adjust Z live while watching the extrusion until it looks correct (Chapter 3), then save. This is the method that converges fastest because it optimises the thing you actually care about — the printed result — rather than a proxy.

> Trick: record your Z-offset in your notes with the build plate it belongs to. Every plate has a different thickness, and swapping a smooth PEI sheet for a textured one changes the correct offset by an amount you will otherwise rediscover by scraping the plate.

Step 4: mesh bed levelling, and its limits

A probe measures the bed at a grid of points and builds a height map. During printing, the firmware adds a small Z correction as a function of X and Y so the nozzle follows the bed's actual shape.

Probe technologies differ in what they physically sense:

| Probe type | Senses | Strengths | Limitations ||---|---|---|---|| Inductive (proximity sensor) | proximity of a conductive/ferrous target | no moving parts, fast, cheap | only works over a metal bed; readings drift with the sensor's own temperature; senses the metal plate, not the print surface on top of it || BLTouch / CRTouch style | mechanical contact of a deployed pin | works over any surface material, well understood | deploying mechanism is a moving part that eventually fails; has an X/Y offset from the nozzle, so it cannot probe the bed edges || Eddy current | distance to a conductive plate, continuously | very fast, can scan while moving, no deployment | needs temperature compensation and calibration against a known reference; conductive plate required || Strain gauge / load cell | the force when the nozzle itself touches the bed | zero X/Y offset because the probe point is the nozzle; senses the real print surface | sensitive to what is on the nozzle tip; needs a clean nozzle every time |

Whatever the type, measure its repeatability:  in Klipper,  in Marlin. Probe the same point ten times and look at the standard deviation. If repeatability is worse than a few tens of micrometres, your mesh is noise and you should fix that before trusting it.

What auto-levelling does not fix

This list saves more time than anything else in the chapter.

- It does not set your Z-offset. A mesh describes the bed's shape; the offset describes the gap. You still set the gap yourself.- It does not fix a tilted bed. Compensation is applied by moving Z during motion, which skews the part's geometry and, on a badly tilted bed, can exceed the compensation range or run the Z motor at absurd speeds. Tram first, compensate second.- It does not fix a bed that moves. If the bed rocks, the mesh you measured is not the bed you print on.- It does not fix a warped plate beyond mesh resolution. A sharp local dent between probe points is invisible to a 5×5 grid.- It does not fix a dirty plate. Grease under the first layer defeats any Z value.- It does not fix an unsquare gantry. If X sags in the middle, the correction changes as Z increases, and a mesh probed at Z ≈ 0 does not know that.- It does not persist automatically. Check whether your start G-code actually loads the mesh ( / ). A probed-but-not-loaded mesh is a common silent failure.

Chapter 2 — Build surfaces and adhesion

The surfaces

| Surface | Adhesion mechanism | Grip | Release | Best for | Watch out ||---|---|---|---|---|---|| Smooth PEI (spring steel) | chemical/polar interaction with the hot polymer | strong | good once cool, by flexing the sheet | PLA, PETG with a release agent, glossy first-layer finish | PETG can bond hard enough to tear the PEI off; use a glue-stick release layer || Textured PEI (powder-coated) | mechanical keying into the texture | strong but distributed | excellent — parts often pop free as the bed cools | the general-purpose default; PLA, PETG, TPU, ABS/ASA | texture is imprinted into the part's bottom face; hides first-layer imperfections, which cuts both ways when diagnosing || Glass | mostly adhesive-assisted; very flat | weak bare, strong with glue/hairspray | excellent as glass cools | perfectly flat bottom faces | heavy, can chip; needs an adhesive for most materials || Garolite / FR4 | mechanical + surface chemistry | strong for nylon specifically | good | nylon (PA), which sticks to garolite and to little else | mediocre for PLA || Adhesive sheets (PET/BuildTak-style) | tack | strong | varies | budget option, replaceable | consumable; gouges easily |

Cleaning: soap and water beats IPA

Isopropyl alcohol is a fine solvent for the last print's release agent, but it is a poor degreaser for skin oils — it dissolves them and then, as it flashes off, redeposits a thin film across the whole plate. Washing the removable sheet in a sink with warm water and ordinary dish soap, then drying with a clean paper towel, physically carries the oil away.

Practical regime:

- Every few prints: wipe with IPA (99 % if you can get it; the water in 70 % dries slowly).- When adhesion drops, or every 10–20 prints: soap and water wash in the sink.- Never: aggressive solvents, abrasive scourers, or metal tools on PEI. Scrape stuck plastic with a plastic scraper, or reheat the bed and let the plastic release.

Why fingerprints matter

PEI's adhesion is chemical. Skin oil is a non-polar film sitting between the polymer and the plate — it is a release agent, and a very effective one. A single fingerprint in the middle of the plate produces a print that adheres everywhere except a palm-sized region, and lifts there. Handle the sheet by its edges. This is not fussiness; it is the most common cause of "my bed suddenly stopped working".

Chapter 3 — Reading a first-layer test print

Print a large single-layer patch — most slicers can do this, and dedicated first-layer test models are common. Look at it in raking light and, if in doubt, put a caliper on it.

Too high (gap too large)

- Individual extrusion lines are visibly separate, with valleys between them; you can see the plate through the gaps.- Each road is round-topped rather than flat-topped.- Poor adhesion: the patch can be lifted with a fingernail, or curls at the corners.- The surface feels ribbed.- Measured thickness is approximately the commanded first-layer height (no squish) or slightly less.

Too low (gap too small)

- Roads are pressed so wide they overlap, and excess material is pushed up as ridges between the lines.- Surface looks translucent, burnished or scratched; on textured plates the texture is smeared.- Audible scraping, and a visible line on the plate.- Extrusion becomes inconsistent because back-pressure is fighting the extruder — sometimes with under-extrusion appearing several centimetres later.- Downstream: pronounced elephant foot on real parts.- Measured thickness is noticeably below the commanded first-layer height.

Correct

- Adjacent roads have merged into a continuous sheet with no gaps and no raised ridges.- Line boundaries are faintly visible but the surface is flat.- The patch is uniformly matte (textured plate) or uniformly glossy (smooth plate) — uniformity is the signal, not the finish itself.- The patch peels off the cooled plate in one piece with moderate effort and shows a clean, evenly-textured underside.- Caliper reading is close to, and slightly under, the commanded first-layer height.

> Common pitfall: judging the first layer at the centre of the plate only. Check all four corners and the centre. Uniformly wrong is a Z-offset problem; wrong in one region only is a tram/mesh problem. They have completely different fixes.

Chapter 4 — The calibration sequence

Do these in order. Each step's result depends on all the previous ones, so a step done out of order will have to be redone.

4.0 — Prerequisites

Mechanical checks from Chapter 1, a trammed bed, a clean plate, dry filament, and a known-good vendor profile as the starting point. Do not begin with a profile you found on the internet.

4.1 — Extruder rotation distance / e-steps

What it fixes: the machine delivering a different length of filament than it was asked for. Everything downstream is scaled by this error, so it is first.

Procedure:

1. Heat the nozzle to printing temperature (the extruder must be able to actually push).2. Put the extruder in relative mode and disable any modifiers.3. Mark the filament at a known distance above the extruder inlet — 120 mm is convenient because it leaves 20 mm after a 100 mm extrusion.4. Command a slow 100 mm extrusion. Slow matters: fast extrusion can skip or grind and will corrupt the measurement.5. Measure the remaining distance from the inlet to the mark with calipers. Actual extruded = 120 − remaining.6. Repeat three times and average.

Applying the result:

Note the inversion: e-steps are steps per millimetre, rotation distance is millimetres per rotation, so the correction goes the opposite way. Getting this backwards doubles your error, which is an obvious enough result that you will notice.

Acceptance: repeat the test after applying. Actual extruded should land within roughly a millimetre of 100 mm. If it does not converge, the drive gear is slipping, the tension is wrong, or the filament path has a restriction.

Scope: this is a per-printer value. It does not change with filament brand.

4.2 — Temperature tower

What it fixes: picking a nozzle temperature by evidence rather than by the number printed on the spool.

Procedure: print a tower whose temperature drops (or rises) by 5 °C per block, using either a pre-built model with embedded temperature changes or a slicer height-triggered custom G-code ( at the layer where each block starts).

What you evaluate on each block, and what each tells you:

| Observation | Meaning ||---|---|| Layer separation when you try to snap the block | too cold — insufficient interdiffusion (post 00, Chapter 3) || Stringing between features | too hot, or a retraction problem (isolate later, not now) || Blobbing, drooping overhangs, glossy over-melted surface | too hot || Poor bridging, curled bridge strands | too cold, or too little cooling || Rough, matte, under-fused surface | too cold |

Decision rule: do not just pick the prettiest block. Pick for the property the part needs. Functional part → the hottest temperature that does not string, droop or degrade, because layer bonding improves with temperature. Visual part → the coolest temperature that still fuses cleanly. Write the choice and the reason down.

Scope: per material, per spool. Pigment loading alone shifts the optimum; a black and a white PLA from the same manufacturer regularly want different temperatures.

4.3 — Flow / extrusion multiplier

What it fixes: the difference between the volume the slicer thinks it deposited and the volume actually deposited. Causes include filament diameter variation, nozzle wear, and material-specific die swell.

Procedure (single-wall cube):

1. Slice a simple cube (or the standard single-wall calibration cube) with one perimeter, zero top layers, zero infill, and no gap fill. Vase mode also works.2. Note the extrusion width the slicer used for that wall — this is the target, and it is not the nozzle diameter (post 02 explains why).3. Print it at your chosen temperature.4. Measure the wall thickness with calipers at eight or more places — different heights, different faces, avoiding the seam and the corners. Use light, consistent pressure; plastic deforms under a caliper jaw.5. Average the measurements.

Applying the result:

Acceptance: reprint and remeasure. The spread between your individual measurements tells you how much precision you actually have — if measurements vary by more than a couple of hundredths of a millimetre, your measurement technique or the machine is the limiting factor, not the flow number.

Modern slicers ship guided flow calibrations (OrcaSlicer's two-pass flow-rate test, for example) that use a printed surface-quality judgement instead of a caliper. They are faster and less prone to caliper-pressure error; the single-wall method remains the one that produces a number you can defend.

Scope: per spool. This is the setting most worth re-checking on every new roll.

4.4 — Retraction

What it fixes: oozing during travel moves, which shows as stringing, blobs and zits.

Procedure: print a retraction tower or a two-pillar stringing test, stepping one variable per tower. Tune in this order: distance, then speed, then extra restart distance / wipe / Z-hop if still needed.

Typical starting ranges — these depend on melt-zone volume, extruder architecture and Bowden tube length, so treat them as where to begin the tower, not as answers:

| Setup | Retraction distance, typical starting range ||---|---|| Direct drive | roughly 0.5–2 mm || Bowden | roughly 3–7 mm, longer with a longer tube |

Acceptance: the shortest retraction distance that eliminates strings. Over-retraction is not free: it pulls molten material back into the cold zone where it can jam, grinds a groove into the filament on repeated retractions at the same point, and causes gaps at the start of each extrusion.

> Watch out: stringing has three plausible causes — too little retraction, too high a temperature, and wet filament. Wet filament strings and pops audibly and will defeat every retraction value you try. If you hear crackling from the nozzle, dry the filament before tuning anything.

Scope: per material.

4.5 — Pressure advance / linear advance

What it fixes: the lag between commanded flow and actual flow caused by pressure building and releasing in the melt zone. Symptoms are bulges after a deceleration and thin, starved lines after an acceleration — most visible at corners and at the start of each line.

Klipper calls it pressure advance; Marlin calls it linear advance with a K factor. Same physics, different scaling, so the numbers are not interchangeable.

Procedure (Klipper): use the tuning-tower mechanism, which ramps the parameter continuously with height:

Print the standard tuning tower object, then find the height where corners are sharpest and line width is most uniform through the corner. The value is  — with the factor above, a good band at 8 mm means a pressure advance of 0.04.

Procedure (Marlin): use the K-factor test pattern (Marlin publishes a generator) and set with . Read the pattern for the K where the fast-to-slow transition shows no bulge and the slow-to-fast transition shows no gap.

Reported typical values span a wide range and depend strongly on architecture — direct-drive setups sit far lower than Bowden ones, because Bowden systems store much more compliance. Do not carry a number across from a different machine; run the tower.

Scope: per material (and re-check if you change hotend or extruder).

4.6 — Maximum volumetric flow

What it fixes: the ceiling on print speed. Above it, the hotend cannot melt filament fast enough and the print silently under-extrudes.

Procedure: print a flow-ramp test that increases volumetric flow with height — OrcaSlicer's "Max Volumetric Speed" test does this directly, and equivalent speed towers exist for other slicers. A typical sweep starts around 5 mm³/s and climbs in small steps.

Reading it: find the height where the surface changes character — extrusion becomes visibly inconsistent, the finish shifts from even to rough or unevenly glossy, or the wall thins. That height's flow is the point of failure, not the working limit.

Applying the result: set your profile's maximum volumetric speed 10–20 % below the failure point. The test is a best case: it does not include retractions, sharp corners, small layers or an already-hot enclosure.

Dependency: this number moves with nozzle temperature. If you later raise the temperature, the ceiling rises too — and if you re-run 4.2, re-run this.

Scope: per material, per hotend, per nozzle.

4.7 — Cooling

What it fixes: overhang quality, bridging, small-feature definition — traded directly against layer adhesion (post 00, Chapter 3).

Procedure: print an overhang/bridging test at several fan settings, or a small tower where fan speed steps with height. Also set minimum layer time (or "slow down for short layers"), which forces small cross-sections to print slowly enough to solidify.

Starting points by material, all subject to measurement:

| Material | Part cooling, typical starting point | Reason ||---|---|---|| PLA | high, often near maximum | crystallises quickly; benefits from fast solidification; layer adhesion stays adequate || PETG | moderate | needs some cooling for overhangs, but too much visibly weakens layer bonding || ABS / ASA | minimal or none, in an enclosure | high shrinkage; rapid cooling causes warping and delamination || TPU | low to moderate | slow-flowing; cooling helps definition but the material is tolerant || Nylon (PA) | low | strong, hygroscopic, and very sensitive to rapid cooling || PC | low | high glass transition; needs a warm environment to bond |

> Trick: cooling is the one setting where "more is better" is actively wrong for functional parts. If a bracket keeps snapping along a layer line, drop the fan before you change anything else.

Scope: per material.

Chapter 5 — Discipline: per spool, and one variable at a time

Why calibration is per spool, not per printer

Split your settings into two buckets:

| Belongs to the printer | Belongs to the spool / material ||---|---|| e-steps / rotation distance | nozzle temperature || XY and Z steps per mm | bed temperature || input shaper frequencies | flow / extrusion multiplier || bed mesh | retraction distance and speed || probe offsets | pressure advance || kinematic limits (max accel, jerk/square corner velocity) | maximum volumetric flow || — | cooling and minimum layer time |

The right-hand column moves because filament varies. Diameter tolerance differs between manufacturers and between batches. Pigments and fillers change melt viscosity, thermal conductivity and optimum temperature. Moisture content changes everything at once. Even two spools of nominally identical material from one vendor can want a different flow number.

Practically: create a slicer filament profile per spool type you buy, not per material category, and put the measured numbers in it.

The tuning-order rule

Never chase two variables at once. If you change temperature and flow together and the result improves, you have learned nothing you can reuse. The sequence in Chapter 4 is ordered by dependency: rotation distance scales all extrusion, temperature changes flow behaviour and the flow ceiling, flow changes how corners look, pressure advance is judged on corner appearance, and cooling changes both bonding and overhang quality.

Keep a notebook — a text file is fine. For each change record: date, printer, spool, the setting changed, the old value, the new value, the test used, and the observation. Six months later this file is the only thing that distinguishes "my printer got worse" from "I changed something in March".

Chapter 6 — Common first-print failures

| Symptom | Most likely causes | First thing to check ||---|---|---|| First layer not sticking | Z-offset too high; greasy plate; bed too cold; unsuitable surface for the material; draught | Wash the plate with soap and water, then print a first-layer patch and read it per Chapter 3 || Elephant foot (bulged bottom edge) | Z-offset too low; bed temperature too high; heavy part squashing a still-soft first layer | Raise Z-offset a few hundredths; enable the slicer's elephant-foot compensation only after fixing the offset || Warping / corners lifting | material shrinkage (ABS, ASA, PC, nylon) plus insufficient adhesion or cool ambient air | Enclosure or draught shield; raise bed temperature; add a brim; reduce part cooling || Stringing | wet filament; temperature too high; insufficient retraction; long travel moves over open space | Listen for crackling and check filament dryness before changing retraction || Under-extrusion (thin, gappy walls) | flow ceiling exceeded; partial clog; extruder slipping; flow multiplier too low; nozzle worn or filament diameter mis-set | Slow the print by half — if it improves, it is a flow-rate/clog problem, not a settings problem || Layer shifts | belt slipping or loose pulley grub screw; acceleration beyond what the mechanics support; nozzle collided with a warped part; stepper driver overheating | Check the pulley grub screws on the shifted axis; then belt tension; then reduce acceleration || Clogs | wet or contaminated filament; heat creep in an all-metal hotend; over-retraction; debris; PTFE liner degraded | Cold pull; check the heatsink fan actually spins; reduce retraction distance || Zits and blobs on the surface | seam placement; pressure advance untuned; retraction restart error | Set seam to "aligned" or "rear" and tune pressure advance (post 02 covers seams) || Rough/inconsistent top surface | under-extrusion; too few top layers; flow multiplier low | Increase top solid thickness, then re-check flow || Ringing / ghosting after corners | mechanical resonance excited by acceleration | Measure resonance with an accelerometer and enable input shaping; check belt tension |

Chapter 7 — Firmware and maintenance

Firmware landscape, at orientation level

| Firmware | Where the work happens | Configuration | Notable for ||---|---|---|---|| Marlin | entirely on the printer's microcontroller | compile-time C++ headers (, ); reflash to change most things | the reference implementation; runs on an enormous range of boards; recent versions include input shaping || Klipper | kinematics and planning on a host computer (usually an SBC); the MCU only executes precisely timed steps | a plain-text , editable live, no reflash for config changes | resonance measurement with an accelerometer, input shaping, pressure advance, macros; the strongest tuning toolset || RepRapFirmware | on the controller board (Duet and compatibles) | G-code macro configuration plus a web interface | strong on multi-tool and industrial-style setups || Vendor forks | on the printer | vendor UI, sometimes closed | Prusa maintains a well-documented Marlin fork; many consumer machines ship a fork whose sources may or may not be published. Check before buying if firmware freedom matters (post 00, Chapter 7) |

The practical question is not "which is best" but "can I measure and change what I need to measure and change". Klipper is popular among people who tune because it makes every value in Chapter 4 adjustable from a text file and re-loadable in seconds.

Maintenance schedule

| Interval | Task ||---|---|| Every print | Confirm the plate is clean and the first layer looks right; confirm the model is where you think it is || Weekly (or per 20 h) | Wipe the plate; check the nozzle for accumulated plastic; visually check belts and the filament path || Monthly | Soap-and-water wash the plate; check belt tension; check pulley grub screws; check that the hotend heatsink fan spins freely; check bed screws for rock || Quarterly | Clean and lightly re-lubricate linear rails or rods with the manufacturer's recommended lubricant; check for Z-lead-screw binding; re-check e-steps; check nozzle wear if you print composites || On material change | Re-check temperature, flow, retraction, pressure advance, cooling for the new spool || On hardware change (nozzle, hotend, extruder, plate) | Re-run the affected calibrations; a new plate always means a new Z-offset || Annually, or on symptoms | Replace the nozzle; inspect PTFE liner if fitted; inspect wiring at flex points, especially the heated-bed cable |

> Watch out: do not lubricate belts, and do not put grease on a bed-adhesion surface or on the nozzle. Also do not over-lubricate rails: excess lubricant collects dust and becomes an abrasive paste.

15:T3f81,A regular expression (regex) is a tiny language for describing text patterns: "four digits, a dash, two digits", "a line that starts with ", "the same word twice in a row". You meet regexes in , , editors, Python, JavaScript, PowerShell and every log tool. This post builds them up from single characters to lookarounds, shows where the flavors differ, gives recipes you will reuse, and ends with the dark side: a regex that can freeze a server.

> Series map: 01 Text Encoding → … → 05 Command Line → 06 Regex (this post) → 07 HTTPS → … → 24 Desk Health. Part B · Everyday skills.

Learning Outcome

- Read and write patterns with literals, character classes, quantifiers, anchors, groups and alternation.- Explain greedy vs lazy matching, backreferences, named groups, and lookahead/lookbehind.- Use flags (ignore case, multiline, dot-all, verbose) in Python, JavaScript and PowerShell.- Know what changes between POSIX BRE/ERE, PCRE, Python , JavaScript and .NET.- Use Python's  module with raw strings, and apply regexes to logs, dates, frontmatter and file names.- Explain why a regex should not fully validate email addresses.- Recognize catastrophic backtracking (ReDoS) and fix it.

Chapter 1 — What a regex is, and how to test one

A regex engine walks through the text and tries to match your pattern at each position. Most characters match themselves. A few metacharacters have special jobs:              .

Test patterns before you trust them. Two good ways:

1. regex101.com — paste a pattern and sample text, pick the flavor (PCRE2, ECMAScript, Python, Golang, Java, .NET, Rust), and it explains every token and shows every match.2. A Python prompt:

Chapter 4 — Quantifiers, greedy and lazy

means 0 or more,  1 or more,  0 or 1 (optional),  exactly 3,  2 or more,  2 to 5. A quantifier applies to the thing right before it:  is  then one or more ;  repeats .

Greedy vs lazy. By default, quantifiers are greedy: they take as much as they can, then give back only if the rest of the pattern fails. Add  to make them lazy: take as little as possible.

> Trick: "anything up to the next " is best written as . It cannot run past the first , so it never needs to backtrack.

Lookarounds check what is next to the current position without consuming it. They match a position, like  does.

Chapter 12 — Don't fully validate email with a regex

Real email syntax (RFC 5322) allows quoted local parts, comments and other rare forms, so a "complete" regex is huge, hard to read and still does not tell you whether the mailbox exists. The HTML standard itself does not follow RFC 5322 for : it calls its own simpler rule a "willful violation" of that RFC, because the RFC grammar is too strict in some places and too loose in others to be practical.

A sensible approach:

1. Do a light sanity check: one , no spaces, a dot in the domain part.2. Send a confirmation email. Only a click on the link proves the address works.

This check rejects some legal but odd addresses (such as ) and accepts some that bounce. That is fine: its only job is to catch typos early.

Chapter 13 — Catastrophic backtracking and ReDoS

Most engines (Python, JavaScript, .NET, PCRE, Java) are backtracking engines. When part of a pattern fails, they go back and try the other ways the earlier parts could have matched. Usually there are only a few ways. But nested or overlapping quantifiers create an exponential number of ways, and on text that almost matches, the engine tries them all.

The textbook case is  against . The inner and outer  can split the s in about 2ⁿ ways, and the final  makes every split fail. A timing on one Windows 11 laptop with Python 3.11.9 (one run each — an informal measurement, not a benchmark; your numbers will differ, the doubling will not):

|  (number of s) | 18 | 20 | 22 | 24 ||---|---|---|---|---|| Time | 0.012 s | 0.055 s | 0.221 s | 0.802 s |

Every 2 extra characters cost about 4 times more. Extrapolating, 40 characters would take more than ten hours. That is ReDoS (regular expression denial of service): one short, crafted input keeps a CPU core busy. OWASP lists typical "evil" shapes: , , , . Real-life versions look innocent, like  for "words separated by spaces" — in the same test, 22 letters plus a  took 0.25 s.

This is not theory. On 2 July 2019, one Cloudflare WAF rule with a badly backtracking regex (the part ) exhausted CPUs across Cloudflare's network and caused about 27 minutes of downtime.

Fixes, from best to last resort

1. Rewrite the pattern so each character can be matched only one way.

| Evil | Safe | Why ||------|------|-----||  |  | the nesting added nothing ||  |  | same language, one way to split ||  |  | each repeat must end with a space, so splits are fixed |

On 100,000 characters, the rewritten  finished in about 1 ms in the same test. It also gave the same yes/no answer as the original on every test string tried (, , , , , ).

2. Forbid backtracking into a part with an atomic group  or a possessive quantifier (, , ). Once matched, the engine will not try other splits. Python supports both since 3.11; PCRE and Java support both; .NET supports atomic groups only; JavaScript supports neither.

3. Use a linear-time engine for untrusted patterns or input. Google's RE2, Go's  and Rust's  crate guarantee time linear in the input size (Rust documents O(m × n) for pattern size m and text size n). The price: no backreferences and no lookarounds. .NET 7 and later offer  with the same kind of guarantee; Windows PowerShell 5.1 runs on .NET Framework and does not have it. After its outage, Cloudflare planned to move to RE2 or the Rust engine for exactly this reason.

4. Limit the damage. Cap input length before matching. In .NET, pass a timeout — the default is infinite:

Python's built-in  has no timeout, so rewrite the pattern or run risky matching in a separate process you can stop.

16:T6929,"The area under the curve" is a picture, not a definition. This post turns it into one. You trap the area between an upper sum that overestimates it and a lower sum that underestimates it, and call the function integrable when the trap can be closed as tightly as you like. From that single idea come proofs that every continuous function and every monotone function is integrable, a function that is not integrable (the Dirichlet function), a surprising one that is (Thomae's function), and both halves of the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, the result that links this post to the derivatives of post 03. The post ends with improper integrals, Lebesgue's exact criterion for Riemann integrability, and the reason a better integral had to be invented.

> Series map: 00 The Real Numbers & Completeness → 01 Sequences & Series → 02 Topology of ℝ & Continuity → 03 Differentiation & Taylor's Theorem → 04 The Riemann Integral (this post) → 05 Uniform Convergence & Power Series.

Learning Outcome

- Define partitions, upper and lower Darboux sums, and the upper and lower integrals of a bounded function.- Prove the refinement lemma and deduce that every lower sum is at most every upper sum.- State and prove the Riemann criterion: $f$ is integrable if and only if $U(f,P) - L(f,P)$ can be made smaller than any $\varepsilon > 0$.- Compute $\int0^1 x^2\,dx$ from the definition, with exact upper and lower sums.- Prove that continuous functions and monotone functions on $[a, b]$ are integrable.- Show that the Dirichlet function is not integrable and that Thomae's function is integrable with integral $0$.- Prove linearity, monotonicity and the triangle inequality for integrals.- State and prove both parts of the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, and use integration by parts and substitution.- Decide convergence of the improper integrals $\int1^\infty x^{-p}\,dx$ and $\int0^1 x^{-p}\,dx$.- State Lebesgue's criterion and explain why the Lebesgue integral exists.

Chapter 1 — Partitions and Darboux sums

Terminology

- Bounded function — throughout, $f : [a, b] \to \mathbb{R}$ with $a < b$ and $\lvert f(x)\rvert \leq B$ for all $x$. Riemann integration is only defined for bounded functions on closed, bounded intervals; everything else is handled by improper integrals (Chapter 6).- Partition — a finite set $P = \{x0, x1, \dots, xn\}$ with $a = x0 < x1 < \dots < xn = b$. The $k$-th subinterval is $[x{k-1}, xk]$, of length $\Delta xk = xk - x{k-1}$.- Mesh — the length of the longest subinterval, $\maxk \Delta xk$.- Refinement — $Q$ refines $P$ if $P \subseteq Q$ (it keeps every point of $P$ and may add more). The common refinement of $P1$ and $P2$ is $P1 \cup P2$.- Local sup and inf — $Mk = \sup\{f(x) : x \in [x{k-1}, xk]\}$ and $mk = \inf\{f(x) : x \in [x{k-1}, xk]\}$. These exist because $f$ is bounded (completeness, post 00).- Upper and lower sums —

$$U(f, P) = \sum{k=1}^{n} Mk\,\Delta xk, \qquad L(f, P) = \sum{k=1}^{n} mk\,\Delta xk.$$

Since $mk \leq Mk$, $L(f, P) \leq U(f, P)$ for every partition. Picture tall rectangles that cover the graph and short rectangles that sit under it.

The refinement lemma

Lemma. If $P \subseteq Q$, then

$$L(f, P) \leq L(f, Q) \leq U(f, Q) \leq U(f, P).$$

Refining can only raise lower sums and lower upper sums.

Proof. By induction it is enough to add one point $z$ to $P$, inside $[x{k-1}, xk]$. Only the $k$-th term of the upper sum changes. It is replaced by

$$M'\,(z - x{k-1}) + M''\,(xk - z),$$

where $M'$ and $M''$ are the sups of $f$ over $[x{k-1}, z]$ and $[z, xk]$. A sup over a smaller set is no bigger, so $M', M'' \leq Mk$, and the new term is at most $Mk(xk - x{k-1})$. So $U(f, Q) \leq U(f, P)$. The argument for lower sums is the same with infs, which can only go up. $\blacksquare$

Corollary. For any two partitions $P1, P2$: $L(f, P1) \leq U(f, P2)$.

Proof. With the common refinement $Q = P1 \cup P2$: $L(f, P1) \leq L(f, Q) \leq U(f, Q) \leq U(f, P2)$. $\blacksquare$

Upper and lower integrals

Every lower sum is a lower bound for the set of upper sums, so by completeness the following exist and satisfy $L(f,P) \leq \underline{\int} f \leq \overline{\int} f \leq U(f,P)$ for every $P$:

$$\underline{\inta^b} f = \supP L(f, P), \qquad \overline{\inta^b} f = \infP U(f, P).$$

Definition. A bounded $f$ is (Riemann) integrable on $[a, b]$ if $\underline{\inta^b} f = \overline{\inta^b} f$. The common value is $\inta^b f$ or $\inta^b f(x)\,dx$.

> Watch out: this is the Darboux form of the definition. The Riemann-sum form picks a sample point $tk$ in each subinterval and asks that $\sum f(tk)\Delta xk$ tend to a limit as the mesh tends to $0$. The two definitions give the same integrable functions and the same values (Bartle and Sherbert, Section 7.4). The Darboux form is easier to prove things with, because upper and lower sums do not depend on any choice of points.

Chapter 2 — The Riemann criterion and a worked example

Theorem (Riemann criterion). A bounded $f$ is integrable on $[a, b]$ if and only if for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is a partition $P$ with

$$U(f, P) - L(f, P) < \varepsilon.$$

Proof. ($\Leftarrow$) For any such $P$,

$$0 \leq \overline{\int} f - \underline{\int} f \leq U(f, P) - L(f, P) < \varepsilon.$$

The difference is a non-negative number smaller than every $\varepsilon > 0$, so it is $0$.

($\Rightarrow$) Let $I$ be the common value and $\varepsilon > 0$. Since $I = \infP U$, there is $P1$ with $U(f, P1) < I + \varepsilon/2$. Since $I = \supP L$, there is $P2$ with $L(f, P2) > I - \varepsilon/2$. For the common refinement $P = P1 \cup P2$ the refinement lemma gives

$$U(f, P) - L(f, P) \leq U(f, P1) - L(f, P2) < \varepsilon. \qquad \blacksquare$$

The criterion is what you use in practice: you never compute a sup over all partitions, you exhibit one good partition for each $\varepsilon$.

Worked example: the integral of x squared on [0, 1]

Let $f(x) = x^2$ and let $Pn$ be the uniform partition $xk = k/n$, $k = 0, \dots, n$, so every $\Delta xk = 1/n$. Since $f$ is increasing on $[0, 1]$, on $[x{k-1}, xk]$ the inf is at the left end and the sup at the right end:

$$mk = \left(\frac{k-1}{n}\right)^2, \qquad Mk = \left(\frac{k}{n}\right)^2.$$

Using $\sum{k=1}^{n} k^2 = \frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}$:

$$U(f, Pn) = \frac{1}{n^3}\sum{k=1}^{n} k^2 = \frac{(n+1)(2n+1)}{6n^2}, \qquad L(f, Pn) = \frac{1}{n^3}\sum{k=1}^{n} (k-1)^2 = \frac{(n-1)(2n-1)}{6n^2}.$$

Subtracting,

$$U(f, Pn) - L(f, Pn) = \frac{1}{n^3}\bigl(n^2 - 0^2\bigr) = \frac{1}{n},$$

because the two sums share every term except the largest square $n^2$ and the smallest $0^2$. Given $\varepsilon > 0$, any $n > 1/\varepsilon$ works, so $f$ is integrable. Both sums tend to $\frac{2}{6} = \frac13$, and $L(f, Pn) \leq \int0^1 x^2\,dx \leq U(f, Pn)$ for every $n$, so

$$\int0^1 x^2\,dx = \frac13.$$

| $n$ | $L(f, Pn)$ | $U(f, Pn)$ | $U - L$ ||---|---|---|---|| 4 | $7/32 = 0.21875$ | $15/32 = 0.46875$ | $1/4$ || 10 | $57/200 = 0.285$ | $77/200 = 0.385$ | $1/10$ || 100 | $0.32835$ | $0.33835$ | $1/100$ |

> Trick: for an increasing function on a uniform partition, the gap rectangles stack into a single column of width $\frac{b-a}{n}$ and height $f(b) - f(a)$. So $U - L = \frac{(f(b) - f(a))(b - a)}{n}$ at once. That is the whole proof of the next chapter's monotone theorem.

Chapter 3 — Which functions are integrable?

Monotone functions

Theorem. If $f$ is monotone on $[a, b]$, then $f$ is integrable.

Proof. Say $f$ is increasing (it is then bounded by $f(a)$ and $f(b)$). On the uniform partition $Pn$, $Mk = f(xk)$ and $mk = f(x{k-1})$, so the sum telescopes:

$$U(f, Pn) - L(f, Pn) = \frac{b - a}{n}\sum{k=1}^{n}\bigl(f(xk) - f(x{k-1})\bigr) = \frac{(b - a)\bigl(f(b) - f(a)\bigr)}{n}.$$

This is smaller than any given $\varepsilon$ once $n$ is large. $\blacksquare$

A monotone function may have infinitely many jumps (for instance one at every $1/k$), and it is still integrable.

Continuous functions

Theorem. If $f$ is continuous on $[a, b]$, then $f$ is integrable.

Proof. A continuous function on the compact set $[a, b]$ is bounded (EVT) and uniformly continuous (post 02). Let $\varepsilon > 0$. Choose $\delta > 0$ such that $\lvert x - y\rvert < \delta$ implies $\lvert f(x) - f(y)\rvert < \frac{\varepsilon}{b - a}$. Take any partition $P$ with mesh $< \delta$. On each subinterval, $f$ attains its max and min (EVT again) at points $sk, tk$ with $\lvert sk - tk\rvert < \delta$, so $Mk - mk = f(sk) - f(tk) < \frac{\varepsilon}{b - a}$. Therefore

$$U(f, P) - L(f, P) = \sum{k=1}^{n} (Mk - mk)\,\Delta xk < \frac{\varepsilon}{b - a}\sum{k=1}^{n} \Delta xk = \varepsilon. \qquad \blacksquare$$

> Common pitfall: plain continuity is not enough for this proof. You need one $\delta$ that works on every subinterval at once, which is exactly uniform continuity. That is why the theorem is stated on a closed, bounded interval.

Finitely many discontinuities

Theorem. If $f$ is bounded on $[a, b]$ and continuous except at finitely many points, then $f$ is integrable.

Sketch. Say $\lvert f\rvert \leq B$ and the bad points are $c1, \dots, cm$. Put a small interval of length $\eta$ around each $cj$. Their total contribution to $U - L$ is at most $2B \cdot m\eta$, which is small when $\eta$ is. On the remaining closed intervals $f$ is continuous, so the previous theorem makes their contribution as small as you like. $\blacksquare$

Changing a function at finitely many points does not change whether it is integrable, or the value of the integral.

A function that is not integrable: Dirichlet

Let $D(x) = 1$ if $x \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $D(x) = 0$ otherwise, on $[0, 1]$. Every subinterval of positive length contains both rationals and irrationals (density of $\mathbb{Q}$ and of the irrationals, post 00), so $Mk = 1$ and $mk = 0$ for every $k$ and every partition. Therefore $U(D, P) = 1$ and $L(D, P) = 0$ always, the upper integral is $1$, the lower integral is $0$, and $D$ is not Riemann integrable.

A function that is integrable: Thomae

Thomae's function on $[0, 1]$ is

$$T(x) = \begin{cases} 1/q & \text{if } x = p/q \text{ in lowest terms with } q \geq 1, \\ 0 & \text{if } x \text{ is irrational}, \end{cases}$$

with $T(0) = 1$ (as $0 = 0/1$). It is continuous at every irrational and discontinuous at every rational (post 02). Even so:

Claim. $T$ is integrable on $[0, 1]$ and $\int0^1 T = 0$.

Proof. Every subinterval contains an irrational, so $mk = 0$ and $L(T, P) = 0$ for every $P$. Let $\varepsilon > 0$. The points where $T(x) \geq \varepsilon/2$ are the fractions $p/q$ with $q \leq 2/\varepsilon$; there are finitely many, say $N$. Choose a partition in which each of these $N$ points lies in subintervals of total length less than $\varepsilon/2$ (for example, give each point its own interval of length less than $\frac{\varepsilon}{4N}$; a point on a boundary touches at most two). On those subintervals $Mk \leq 1$, contributing less than $\varepsilon/2$. On all other subintervals $Mk < \varepsilon/2$, contributing at most $\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot 1$. So $U(T, P) < \varepsilon$. By the Riemann criterion $T$ is integrable, and since every lower sum is $0$, the integral is $0$. $\blacksquare$

For a feel of the numbers: with $\varepsilon = \frac15$, the points where $T \geq \frac1{10}$ are the fractions in $[0, 1]$ with denominator at most $10$, and there are exactly $33$ of them.

Chapter 4 — Properties of the integral

Let $f, g$ be integrable on $[a, b]$ and $k \in \mathbb{R}$.

| Property | Statement ||---|---|| Linearity | $f + g$ and $kf$ are integrable; $\int (f + g) = \int f + \int g$ and $\int kf = k\int f$ || Monotonicity | if $f \leq g$ on $[a, b]$ then $\inta^b f \leq \inta^b g$ || Additivity | for $a < c < b$: $f$ integrable on $[a, b]$ iff integrable on $[a, c]$ and $[c, b]$, and $\inta^b f = \inta^c f + \intc^b f$ || Triangle inequality | $\lvert f\rvert$ is integrable and $\lvert \inta^b f\rvert \leq \inta^b \lvert f\rvert$ || Bound | if $m \leq f \leq M$ then $m(b - a) \leq \inta^b f \leq M(b - a)$ || Products | $fg$ is integrable |

Proof of the sum rule. On any subinterval, $\sup(f + g) \leq \sup f + \sup g$ and $\inf(f + g) \geq \inf f + \inf g$. So $U(f + g, P) \leq U(f, P) + U(g, P)$ and $L(f + g, P) \geq L(f, P) + L(g, P)$. Given $\varepsilon$, take a common refinement where both $U - L$ gaps are below $\varepsilon/2$; then the gap for $f + g$ is below $\varepsilon$, and its integral is squeezed between $L(f,P) + L(g,P)$ and $U(f,P) + U(g,P)$, which both lie within $\varepsilon$ of $\int f + \int g$. $\blacksquare$

Proof of monotonicity. $g - f \geq 0$ has every lower sum $\geq 0$, so $\int (g - f) \geq 0$; then use linearity. $\blacksquare$

Proof of the triangle inequality. On any subinterval, for $x, y$ in it, $\bigl\lvert \lvert f(x)\rvert - \lvert f(y)\rvert \bigr\rvert \leq \lvert f(x) - f(y)\rvert$, so the oscillation $\sup\lvert f\rvert - \inf\lvert f\rvert$ is at most $Mk - mk$. Hence $U(\lvert f\rvert, P) - L(\lvert f\rvert, P) \leq U(f, P) - L(f, P)$ and $\lvert f\rvert$ is integrable. Since $-\lvert f\rvert \leq f \leq \lvert f\rvert$, monotonicity gives $-\int\lvert f\rvert \leq \int f \leq \int \lvert f\rvert$. $\blacksquare$

Convention. Define $\intb^a f = -\inta^b f$ and $\inta^a f = 0$. Then the additivity formula holds for any order of $a, b, c$.

Mean Value Theorem for integrals. If $f$ is continuous on $[a, b]$, there is $c \in [a, b]$ with $\inta^b f = f(c)(b - a)$.

Proof. With $m, M$ the min and max of $f$ (EVT), the bound property gives $m \leq \frac{1}{b-a}\inta^b f \leq M$, and the IVT gives a $c$ where $f$ takes this average value. $\blacksquare$

For example, $\int0^2 x^2\,dx = \frac83$, so $c^2 \cdot 2 = \frac83$, $c = \frac{2}{\sqrt 3}$: the same point as the MVT example for $x^3$ in post 03, and that is no coincidence, because $\frac{x^3}{3}$ is an antiderivative of $x^2$.

Chapter 5 — The Fundamental Theorem of Calculus

Part 1: integrating then differentiating

Theorem (FTC, part 1). Let $f$ be integrable on $[a, b]$ and define

$$F(x) = \inta^x f(t)\,dt, \qquad x \in [a, b].$$

Then $F$ is continuous on $[a, b]$ (in fact Lipschitz). If $f$ is continuous at $c \in [a, b]$, then $F$ is differentiable at $c$ and $F'(c) = f(c)$ (one-sided at an endpoint).

Proof. Let $\lvert f\rvert \leq B$. For $x < y$, additivity and the bound give $\lvert F(y) - F(x)\rvert = \left\lvert \intx^y f\right\rvert \leq B(y - x)$. So $F$ is Lipschitz, hence continuous.

Now let $f$ be continuous at $c$ and $\varepsilon > 0$. Choose $\delta$ with $\lvert f(t) - f(c)\rvert < \varepsilon$ for $\lvert t - c\rvert < \delta$. For $0 < \lvert x - c\rvert < \delta$,

$$\frac{F(x) - F(c)}{x - c} - f(c) = \frac{1}{x - c}\intc^x \bigl(f(t) - f(c)\bigr)\,dt,$$

and the right side has absolute value at most $\frac{1}{\lvert x - c\rvert}\cdot \varepsilon \lvert x - c\rvert = \varepsilon$. $\blacksquare$

So every continuous function has an antiderivative. One famous use: define $\ln x = \int1^x \frac{dt}{t}$ for $x > 0$. Then FTC 1 gives $\ln'(x) = \frac1x$ at once.

Worked example. Let $G(x) = \int0^{x^2} \cos(t^2)\,dt$. With $F(u) = \int0^{u} \cos(t^2)\,dt$, FTC 1 gives $F'(u) = \cos(u^2)$, and $G(x) = F(x^2)$. By the chain rule (post 03),

$$G'(x) = \cos\bigl((x^2)^2\bigr)\cdot 2x = 2x\cos(x^4).$$

Part 2: differentiating then integrating

Theorem (FTC, part 2). Let $F$ be differentiable on $[a, b]$ (one-sided at the endpoints) and suppose $f = F'$ is integrable on $[a, b]$. Then

$$\inta^b f = F(b) - F(a).$$

Proof. Take any partition $P$. Apply the Mean Value Theorem (post 03) to $F$ on each $[x{k-1}, xk]$: there is $tk$ in the open subinterval with $F(xk) - F(x{k-1}) = F'(tk)\Delta xk = f(tk)\Delta xk$. Summing, the left side telescopes:

$$F(b) - F(a) = \sum{k=1}^{n} f(tk)\,\Delta xk.$$

Since $mk \leq f(tk) \leq Mk$, this gives $L(f, P) \leq F(b) - F(a) \leq U(f, P)$ for every $P$. So $F(b) - F(a)$ lies between the lower and upper integrals, which are equal. $\blacksquare$

> Watch out: the hypothesis "$F'$ is integrable" cannot be dropped. $F(x) = x^2\sin(1/x^2)$ with $F(0) = 0$ is differentiable on $[-1, 1]$, but $F'(x) = 2x\sin(1/x^2) - \frac{2}{x}\cos(1/x^2)$ for $x \neq 0$ is unbounded near $0$, so it is not Riemann integrable. A derivative can even be bounded and still not Riemann integrable (Volterra's function, 1881).

Integration by parts and substitution

Integration by parts. If $u, v$ are differentiable on $[a, b]$ and $u', v'$ are integrable, then

$$\inta^b u\,v' = u(b)v(b) - u(a)v(a) - \inta^b u'\,v.$$

This is FTC 2 applied to $(uv)' = u'v + uv'$. Example: $\int0^1 x e^x\,dx = \bigl[x e^x\bigr]0^1 - \int0^1 e^x\,dx = e - (e - 1) = 1$.

Substitution. If $\varphi : [a, b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuously differentiable and $f$ is continuous on an interval containing $\varphi([a, b])$, then

$$\inta^b f(\varphi(t))\,\varphi'(t)\,dt = \int{\varphi(a)}^{\varphi(b)} f(u)\,du.$$

Proof: both sides, as functions of the upper limit $b$, have the same derivative (FTC 1 and the chain rule) and agree when $b = a$. Example: with $u = x^2 + 1$, $\int0^1 2x(x^2 + 1)^3\,dx = \int1^2 u^3\,du = \frac{2^4 - 1^4}{4} = \frac{15}{4}$.

Chapter 6 — Improper integrals

The Riemann integral needs a bounded function on a closed, bounded interval. An improper integral relaxes one of these by a limit.

- Unbounded interval. If $f$ is integrable on $[a, R]$ for every $R > a$, define $\inta^\infty f = \lim{R \to \infty} \inta^R f$ when the limit exists (the integral converges).- Unbounded function. If $f$ is integrable on $[a + \eta, b]$ for every small $\eta > 0$, define $\inta^b f = \lim{\eta \to 0^+} \int{a+\eta}^b f$.

The p-test. For $p > 0$:

$$\int1^R x^{-p}\,dx = \begin{cases} \dfrac{1 - R^{1-p}}{p - 1} & p \neq 1, \\ \ln R & p = 1. \end{cases}$$

As $R \to \infty$: if $p > 1$ then $R^{1-p} \to 0$ and the integral converges to $\frac{1}{p - 1}$; if $p \leq 1$ it diverges to $\infty$. Near $0$ the roles swap: $\int0^1 x^{-p}\,dx$ converges (to $\frac{1}{1 - p}$) exactly when $p < 1$.

| Integral | Converges? | Value ||---|---|---|| $\int1^\infty x^{-2}\,dx$ | yes ($p = 2 > 1$) | $1$ || $\int1^\infty x^{-1}\,dx$ | no ($p = 1$) | diverges || $\int0^1 x^{-1/2}\,dx$ | yes ($p = \frac12 < 1$) | $2$ || $\int0^\infty e^{-x}\,dx$ | yes | $1$ |

This mirrors the $p$-series test of post 01: $\sum 1/k^p$ converges exactly when $p > 1$. The integral test makes the link precise: for $f$ positive and decreasing on $[1, \infty)$, $\sum f(k)$ and $\int1^\infty f$ converge or diverge together.

> Common pitfall: $\int{-\infty}^{\infty} f$ is defined as $\int{-\infty}^0 f + \int0^\infty f$ with both limits existing separately. $\lim{R \to \infty}\int{-R}^{R} x\,dx = 0$, but $\int{-\infty}^\infty x\,dx$ diverges.

Chapter 7 — Lebesgue's criterion, and why Lebesgue integration exists

Measure zero

A set $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ has measure zero if for every $\varepsilon > 0$ it can be covered by countably many open intervals whose total length is less than $\varepsilon$.

- Every finite set has measure zero.- Every countable set $\{a1, a2, \dots\}$ has measure zero: cover $ak$ by an interval of length $\varepsilon/2^{k+1}$; the total is at most $\varepsilon/2$. So $\mathbb{Q}$ has measure zero (it is countable, post 00).- An interval $[c, d]$ with $c < d$ does not have measure zero.

Theorem (Lebesgue's criterion). A function $f : [a, b] \to \mathbb{R}$ is Riemann integrable if and only if $f$ is bounded and the set of points where $f$ is discontinuous has measure zero.

This explains every example in the post at once:

| Function | Discontinuities | Integrable? ||---|---|---|| continuous $f$ | none | yes || monotone $f$ | countable (jumps) | yes || Thomae $T$ | the rationals: countable | yes || Dirichlet $D$ | every point of $[0, 1]$ | no || $x^{-1/2}$ on $(0, 1]$ | — but unbounded | not Riemann; only improper |

The proof is in Abbott, Section 7.6, and Rudin, Theorem 11.33.

Why a better integral was needed

The Riemann integral does not behave well under limits. Enumerate the rationals in $[0, 1]$ as $q1, q2, \dots$ and let $fn$ be $1$ at $q1, \dots, qn$ and $0$ elsewhere. Each $fn$ has finitely many discontinuities, so it is integrable with $\int0^1 fn = 0$. The functions increase and are bounded by $1$, and $fn(x) \to D(x)$ at every $x$. But the limit $D$ is not Riemann integrable, so "$\int fn \to \int \lim fn$" is not even meaningful.

Lebesgue's integral (1902) partitions the range of $f$ instead of its domain, and measures the sets where $f$ takes values in each range slice. It integrates $D$ (answer $0$, since $\mathbb{Q}$ has measure zero), agrees with the Riemann integral whenever the latter exists, and satisfies clean limit theorems such as the Monotone and Dominated Convergence Theorems. Post 05 shows the Riemann-world substitute: limits and integrals commute when convergence is uniform.

Practice Exercises

Exercise 1. For $f(x) = x$ on $[0, 1]$ and the uniform partition $Pn$, compute $U(f, Pn)$ and $L(f, Pn)$ and deduce $\int0^1 x\,dx$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

$Mk = k/n$ and $mk = (k-1)/n$, each times $\Delta xk = 1/n$. With $\sum{k=1}^n k = \frac{n(n+1)}{2}$: $U = \frac{n+1}{2n}$ and $L = \frac{n-1}{2n}$. So $U - L = \frac1n \to 0$ and both tend to $\frac12$. Hence $\int0^1 x\,dx = \frac12$.

</details>

Exercise 2. For $f(x) = x^3$ on $[0, 2]$ with the uniform partition into $n$ pieces, show $U - L = \frac{16}{n}$. How large must $n$ be for $U - L < 0.01$?

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

By the trick of Chapter 2 (increasing function, uniform partition), $U - L = \frac{(b - a)(f(b) - f(a))}{n} = \frac{2 \cdot 8}{n} = \frac{16}{n}$. Explicitly, $U = \frac{4(n+1)^2}{n^2}$ and $L = \frac{4(n-1)^2}{n^2}$, whose difference is $\frac{16n}{n^2}$. Then $\frac{16}{n} < 0.01$ needs $n > 1600$, so $n = 1601$ is the smallest. Both sums tend to $4 = \int0^2 x^3\,dx$.

</details>

Exercise 3. Let $f = 0$ on $[0, 1]$ except $f(\frac12) = 5$. Prove from the definition that $f$ is integrable with integral $0$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Every lower sum is $0$ (each subinterval contains points where $f = 0$). Given $\varepsilon > 0$, take $0 < \delta < \min(\frac{\varepsilon}{10}, \frac12)$ and the partition $\{0, \frac12 - \delta, \frac12 + \delta, 1\}$. Only the middle subinterval has $Mk = 5$, so $U = 5 \cdot 2\delta < \varepsilon$. By the Riemann criterion $f$ is integrable, and the integral is squeezed to $0$.

</details>

Exercise 4. Evaluate $\int0^1 \frac{dx}{\sqrt x}$ and decide whether $\int1^\infty \frac{dx}{x}$ converges.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

$\int\eta^1 x^{-1/2}\,dx = 2 - 2\sqrt\eta \to 2$ as $\eta \to 0^+$, so the first integral is $2$. For the second, $\int1^R \frac{dx}{x} = \ln R \to \infty$: it diverges ($p = 1$).

</details>

Exercise 5. Let $f$ be continuous on $[a, b]$ with $f \geq 0$ and $\inta^b f = 0$. Prove $f(x) = 0$ for all $x$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Suppose $f(c) > 0$ for some $c$. By continuity there is an interval $[c', d'] \subseteq [a, b]$ of positive length containing $c$ on which $f \geq \frac{f(c)}{2}$. By additivity and monotonicity, $\inta^b f \geq \int{c'}^{d'} f \geq \frac{f(c)}{2}(d' - c') > 0$. Contradiction. (Continuity is needed: Thomae's function is $\geq 0$, not identically $0$, and has integral $0$.)

</details>

Exercise 6. Find $H'(x)$ for $H(x) = \int{x}^{x^2} \frac{dt}{t}$ on $x > 0$, and explain the answer.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Write $H(x) = \int1^{x^2} \frac{dt}{t} - \int1^{x} \frac{dt}{t}$. By FTC 1 and the chain rule, $H'(x) = \frac{1}{x^2}\cdot 2x - \frac1x = \frac1x$. Indeed $H(x) = \ln(x^2) - \ln x = \ln x$.

</details>

Exercise 7. Explain why the pointwise limit of integrable functions need not be integrable, using the functions $fn$ from Chapter 7.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Each $fn$ is $1$ at $n$ rationals and $0$ elsewhere, so it has $n$ discontinuities and $\int0^1 fn = 0$. At a rational $x = qm$, $fn(x) = 1$ once $n \geq m$; at an irrational $x$, $fn(x) = 0$ for all $n$. So $fn \to D$ pointwise, and $D$ is not Riemann integrable (upper integral 1, lower integral 0). The convergence is not uniform: $\sup\lvert fn - D\rvert = 1$ for every $n$.

</details>

Cheat-sheet

| Idea | Statement ||---|---|| Upper / lower sum | $U = \sum Mk\Delta xk$, $L = \sum mk\Delta xk$ || Refinement | $P \subseteq Q \Rightarrow L(P) \leq L(Q) \leq U(Q) \leq U(P)$ || Integrable | lower integral $=$ upper integral || Riemann criterion | for every $\varepsilon$ some $P$ has $U - L < \varepsilon$ || Increasing, uniform partition | $U - L = (b - a)(f(b) - f(a))/n$ || $x^2$ on $[0,1]$ | $U - L = 1/n$, integral $1/3$ || Always integrable | continuous; monotone; bounded with finitely many discontinuities || Not integrable | Dirichlet (upper 1, lower 0); unbounded functions || Thomae | integrable, integral 0 || Triangle inequality | $\lvert \int f\rvert \leq \int \lvert f\rvert$ || FTC 1 | $F(x) = \inta^x f$ is Lipschitz; $F'(c) = f(c)$ where $f$ is continuous || FTC 2 | $F' = f$ integrable $\Rightarrow \inta^b f = F(b) - F(a)$ || p-test | $\int1^\infty x^{-p}$ converges iff $p > 1$; $\int0^1 x^{-p}$ iff $p < 1$ || Lebesgue criterion | bounded and discontinuities of measure zero |

What's next

Post 05 studies sequences and series of functions. It separates pointwise from uniform convergence, proves that uniform limits of continuous functions are continuous and that integrals commute with uniform limits (the theorem the Dirichlet example above was missing), and then applies everything to power series: radius of convergence, term-by-term differentiation and integration, Abel's theorem and the Weierstrass approximation theorem.

Bartle and Donald R. Sherbert, Introduction to Real Analysis, 4th ed., Wiley, 2011 — Chapter 7 (Riemann sums, the Darboux integral and their equivalence, FTC, Lebesgue's criterion).- Michael Spivak, Calculus, 4th ed., Publish or Perish, 2008 — Chapters 13–14 (integrals and the Fundamental Theorem).- Jiří Lebl, Basic Analysis I: Introduction to Real Analysis, free textbook — jirka.org/ra, Chapter 5.- MIT OpenCourseWare, 18.100A Real Analysis (Fall 2020), lecture notes on the Riemann integral and the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus.

Learning Outcome

- Explain the core difference: Bash pipes text, PowerShell pipes objects.- Redirect stdout and stderr correctly, and avoid PowerShell 5.1's encoding traps.- Search text ( /  / ), find files ( /  / ), and edit in place ( / ).- Count and rank with  and . Query JSON with  or , and call HTTP APIs with  / .- Use environment variables, exit codes, chaining and quoting correctly in both shells.- Search history, set up aliases and profiles, and use SSH keys, ,  and .

Chapter 1 — Two shells, two ideas

Bash passes plain text between programs. Every tool reads lines and writes lines, and you cut columns out of text with ,  or . PowerShell passes .NET objects.  sends file objects with properties like ,  and , so you filter by property, not by column position. "Files over 1 MB" in each shell:

| Bash — parse column 5 of text | PowerShell — filter a property ||------|------||  |  |

On Windows, Git Bash comes with Git for Windows: real Bash plus GNU tools (, , , , …). It shows drives as ;  converts a path to Windows form. Windows PowerShell 5.1 is built into Windows. The newer PowerShell 7 () is a separate install and fixes several traps shown below; this post targets 5.1 because it is always there.

> Watch out: in Windows PowerShell 5.1, , ,  and  are aliases for cmdlets (, , , ). They look like the Unix tools but take different options.  shows what a name really runs.

Terminology

| Term | Meaning ||------|---------|| Shell / terminal | The program that reads your commands (Bash, PowerShell) / the window that hosts it || stdin / stdout / stderr | Standard input, normal output, error output (streams 0, 1, 2) || Pipe  | Sends one command's output into the next command's input || Exit code | Number a program returns: 0 = success, anything else = failure || Cmdlet | A PowerShell command named , e.g.  || Environment variable | A named value every child process inherits, e.g.  |

Chapter 3 — Pipes and redirection

| Goal | Bash | PowerShell 5.1 ||------|------|----------------|| stdout to file (overwrite / append) |  /  |  /  || stderr to file |  |  || Both into one file |  |  || Throw errors away |  |  || Throw everything away |  |  or  || Show and save |  |  |

PowerShell has six redirectable streams (1 success, 2 error, 3 warning, 4 verbose, 5 debug, 6 information);  redirects all of them.

> Common pitfall: order matters in Bash.  sends both streams to the file.  sends stderr to the terminal, because  copies where stdout pointed at that moment.

> Watch out: Windows PowerShell 5.1 does not write UTF-8 by default.  and  write UTF-16LE;  and  write the ANSI code page;  adds a BOM. See Dev Essentials 01 · Text Encoding for safe patterns.

> Watch out: in 5.1, redirecting a native program's stderr () wraps each stderr line in an . It prints like an error, and  becomes , even when the program succeeded (checked on 5.1.26100). Judge native programs by  (Chapter 9).

Chapter 7 — Counting, sorting and watching

The classic ranking pipeline: pull out a field, sort, count duplicates, sort by count.

only merges duplicates that sit next to each other, so the first  is required.  then sorts by number, biggest first. PowerShell groups objects instead (first table row).

Exit codes. 0 means success; anything else means failure.

and  do not exist in Windows PowerShell 5.1. Typing them gives the parser error  They arrived in PowerShell 7 as pipeline chain operators. In 5.1, use  plus an  on  or . Also,  stops on cmdlet errors, but a native program's non-zero exit code still needs an explicit  check.

Chapter 10 — Quoting rules

Rules that save hours:

- Prefer single quotes for regexes, JSON and anything with  in it — in both shells.- Braces are special in PowerShell.  breaks in 5.1; write  (see Dev Essentials 03 · Git Rescue Kit).- The stop-parsing token  passes the rest of the line to a native program almost as-is: . (Backslash is not an escape in PowerShell, so  is just a path.)

Chapter 11 — History, aliases and profiles

History search. In Bash, press Ctrl+R and type part of an old command; press Ctrl+R again for older matches. PowerShell's PSReadLine module (bundled with 5.1) uses the same Ctrl+R, and F8 finds history entries that start with what you already typed. To grep old commands:  in Bash. In PowerShell,  only shows the current session; the full saved history lives in , so search it with .

Aliases and functions.

Profiles. Git Bash starts as a login shell, so it reads . If you only have , Git for Windows prints a warning and creates a  that loads it. Reload after edits with . PowerShell's profile path is in  (for 5.1: ):

> Watch out: the default execution policy on Windows client editions is , which blocks all scripts — profiles included.  allows your own local scripts while still requiring signatures on downloaded ones. Microsoft notes that the policy is a safety net, not a security boundary.

Chapter 12 — SSH: keys, config, copying files

Make a key. Ed25519 is the type GitHub's docs recommend; they keep RSA 4096 only for old systems that lack Ed25519.

Accept the default path and set a passphrase. Two files appear:  (private — never share it) and  (public — paste it into GitHub or a server's ). The  comment is only a label.

Load it into an agent so you type the passphrase once:

On Windows there are two  programs: Git Bash runs its own , and PowerShell runs . Both read the same  folder, but each has its own agent.

Name your servers in :

SSH uses the first value it finds for each setting, so put specific hosts at the top and any  defaults at the bottom. Now  replaces .

copies only what changed. The trailing slash matters:  means "the contents of site",  means "the folder itself".  removes files on the target that are gone from the source, so always preview first. Git Bash does not include ; use it from Linux, macOS or WSL, or fall back to . Test GitHub access with .
